Short Rainbow Circuits in Regular Matroids



Sean McGuinness

Affiliation: Dept. of Mathematics

Affiliation: Thompson Rivers University

Affiliation: McGill Road, Kamloops BC

Affiliation: V2C5N3 Canada

Affiliation: email: smcguinness@tru.ca



Abstract
 

DeVos et al conjectured that if M is a simple, regular matroid and c is a colouring of the elements of M with r(M)+1 colours, where each colour class has at least two elements, then M contains a rainbow circuit of size at most
 \lceil\frac{r(M)+1}{2}\rceil. We prove this conjecture by showing that for all such regular matroids there are four rainbow circuits C_{i},\ i=1,2,3,4 for which \sum_{i}|C_{i}|\leq 2r(M)+4 and for which no element of M belongs to more than two of the circuits.
 

AMS Subject Classifications (2012) : 05D99,05B35.
 




1 Introduction




For any positive integer k , [k] shall denote the set \{1,\dots,k\} and for any integers a<b, [a,b] will denote the set \{a,a+1,\dots,b\}. For any graph G , we let \nu(G) and \varepsilon(G) denote the number of vertices and edges, respectively in G. 




A colouring for a graph G is defined to be a function c:E(G)\rightarrow S for some set S . We refer to the pair (G,c) as a coloured graph. If |S|=k, then we refer to c as a \mathbf{k} - colouring. For a colouring c of a graph G and a subset A\subseteq E(G) , we define c(A):=\{c(e)\ \big|\ e\in A\} and we define c(G):=c(E(G)); that is, the set of distinct colours appearing in G. For a colouring c:E(G)\rightarrow S and for all s\in S we let c^{-1}(s)=\{e\in E(G)\ \big|\ c(e)=s\}. We refer to c^{-1}(s) as the colour class for colour s . For a colouring c of G , we say that a subset A\subseteq E(G) is a rainbow-subset if c restricted to A is injective. A subgraph H of G is a rainbow subgraph if E(H) is a rainbow subset of E(G). A vertex v is said to be a rainbow vertex if the set of edges incident with v form a rainbow subset.




The problem dealt with in this paper has its origins in the
well-known Caccetta-Häggkvist conjecture [4]:









1.1 Conjecture ( Cacetta, Häggkvist )




Every simple directed graph on n vertices with minimum out-degree r contains a directed cycle of length
at most \lceil\frac{n}{r}\rceil .




The authors in [1] observed that if for the directed graph G in the above conjecture, one assigns n colours to the edges whereby the out-directed edges at each vertex receive the same colour, then the conjecture asserts that there
exists a rainbow cycle of length at most \lceil\frac{n}{r}\rceil . As such, they posed the following more general conjecture:









1.2 Conjecture ( Aharoni, Devos, Holzman )




Let G be a graph and let c be a \nu(G) -colouring of G where each colour class has size at least r . Then the coloured graph (G,c) contains a rainbow cycle of length at most \lceil\frac{\nu(G)}{r}\rceil. 




In [6], the authors verified the above conjecture in the case where r=2. In [8], it was shown that Conjecture 1 holds if each colour class has \Omega(r) edges and in [7] the authors show that Conjecture 1 holds up to an additive constant. That is, for all r\geq 2 there is a constant \alpha_{r} such Conjecture 1 is true if one replaces \lceil\frac{\nu(G)}{2}\rceil with \lceil\frac{\nu(G)}{2}\rceil+\alpha_{r}. 




In [6], the authors considered an extension of rainbow colourings to matroids. A colouring of a matroid M is defined to be a function c:E(M)\rightarrow S for some set S . We call the pair (M,c) a coloured matroid. If S=[k] , then we refer to c as a \mathbf{k} - colouring of M. As with graphs, for each s\in S, we define c^{-1}(s)=\{e\in E(M)\ \big|\ c(e)=s\} to be the colour class for the colour s. For a subset A\subset E(M), we define c(A):=\{c(e)\ \big|\ e\in A\} and we define c(M):=c(E(M)), the set of distinct colours appearing in M. We say that an element e in a coloured matroid is colour-singular if e is the only element in the colour class containing it.
A colouring of matroid (or graph) is said to be \mathbf{k} -bounded (resp. \mathbf{k} -uniform) if each colour class has at most (resp. exactly) k elements. If a colour class X contains two elements and e\in X , we shall denote by e^{\prime} the other element in X. 




For a coloured matroid (M,c) , a subset A\subseteq E(M) is said to be a rainbow subset if c restricted to A is injective. In particular, a rainbow circuit is a circuit whose elements all have different colours. We say that a rainbow circuit C in (M,c) is short if |C|\leq\left\lfloor\frac{r(M)+2}{2}\right\rfloor .
A coloured matroid (M,c) is said to be circuit - achromatic if it contains no rainbow circuits. A subset A\subseteq E(M) is said to be monochromatic if all the elements in A have the same colour.




We shall make frequent use of the following observation whose easy proof is left to the reader.









1.3 Observation




Suppose (M,c) is a simple, circuit-achromatic coloured matroid where \varepsilon(M)=2r(M)-1 and c is 2 -bounded. Then (M,c) has exactly one colour-singular element.




Extending Conjecture 1 for the case r=2 to matroids, the authors in [6] posed the following conjecture:






1.4 Conjecture




Let M be a simple, regular matroid and let c be a (r(M)+1) -colouring of M . If (M,c) has no colour-singular elements, then M has a short rainbow circuit.

In [6], the conjecture is shown to be true for graphic and cographic matroids. In this paper, we prove the above conjecture by proving the existence of four rainbow circuits the sum of whose cardinalities is at most 2r(M)+4. 




A cycle in a matroid is a disjoint union of circuits.
For a coloured matroid (M,c) , a pair of circuits (resp. cycles) \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is said to be a rainbow circuit pair (RCP) (resp. rainbow cycle pair (RCyP)) if C_{1} and C_{2} are disjoint rainbow circuits (resp. cycles).
Generally, a disjoint pair of rainbow subsets \{X_{1},X_{2}\} is said to be a rainbow pair.
A RCP (resp. RCyP) \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is said to be short if |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq r(M)+2. In shorthand, we write SRCP (resp. SRCyP) for a short circuit (resp, cycle) pair.




A family of circuits \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}\} is said to be a short rainbow circuit 4 -tuple (SRC \mathbf{4} -tuple) if the circuits C_{i},\ i\in[4] are rainbow circuits for which \sum_{i}|C_{i}|\leq 2r(M)+4 and for which no element of M belongs to more than two of the circuits. We can define the same for cycles where a SRCy 4 -tuple denotes a short rainbow cycle 4 -tuple.
Note that if \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a SRCP (resp. SRCyP), then \{C_{1},C_{1},C_{2},C_{2}\} is a SRC 4 -tuple (resp. SRCy 4 -tuple).
By averaging, one sees that at least one of the circuits in an SRC 4 -tuple is short. We prove Conjecture 1 by proving the following:









1.5 Theorem




Let M be a simple regular matroid and let c be a 2 -uniform (r(M)+1) -colouring of M . Then M has a SRC 4 -tuple.




1.1 Notation




For definitions and notation pertaining to matroids, we shall follow [9].
For a set X and elements x_{1},\dots,x_{k} we shall often write X+x_{1}+\cdots+x_{k} (resp. X-x_{1}-\cdots-x-k ) in place of X\cup\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\} (resp. X\backslash\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\} ).
For a graph G , M(G) will denote the cycle matroid for G , and M^{*}(G) will denote the cocycle matroid. The sets E(G) and V(G) will denote the sets of edges and vertices of G , respectively.
For a set X , either X\subseteq V(G) or X\subseteq E(G), G[X] will denote the subgraph of G induced by X .




For a graph G , let \kappa(G) denote the number of components of G. 
For a vertex v\in V(G) , d_{G}(v) will denote the degree of the vertex v. For a subset U\subseteq V(G), we let d_{G}(U)=\sum_{v\in U}d_{G}(v). The set N_{G}(v) will denote the set of neighbours of v and E_{G}(v) will denote the set of edges incident with v. 
For an edge e\in E(G) we shall write e=uv to mean that u and v are the endvertices of e . For an edge e\in E(G), we let G\backslash e (resp. G/e ) denote the graph obtained from G by deleting (resp. contracting) e. 
For a subgraph H of G , we often write |H| in place of |E(H)| or \varepsilon(H) which we refer to as the size of H. We denote the subgraph obtained from H by adding v and all edges between v and vertices in H by H+v. For an edge e\not\in E(H), we let H+e denote the subgraph obtained by adding e to H , including the endvertices of e. 




Suppose (G,c) is a coloured graph. For vertices u,v\in V(G), we let dist_{G}^{c}(u,v) be the length of a shortest rainbow path between u and v in G ; if no such path exists, we define dist_{G}^{c}(u,v):=\infty. For a coloured graph (G,c) (resp. coloured matroid (M,c) ) and a minor H of G (resp. N of M ), we let c\big|H (resp. c\big|N ) denote the colouring of H (resp. N ) which is the restriction of c to E(H) (resp. E(N) ).




Let P=v_{1}e_{2}v_{2}e_{2}\cdots e_{k}v_{k+1} be a path where v_{1},\dots,v_{k+1} are vertices and e_{1},\dots,e_{k} are edges. If we extend P to a path P^{\prime}=v_{1}e_{2}v_{2}e_{2}\cdots e_{k}v_{k+1}ev or P^{\prime}=vev_{1}e_{2}v_{2}e_{2}\cdots e_{k}v_{k+1} by adding an edge e and a vertex v , then we shall simply write P^{\prime}=P+e+v. 




For a matroid M and a set S where E(M)\cap S=\mathchar 31 we say that a matroid N is a regular (resp. graphic, cographic) extension of M by S if M is regular (resp. graphic, cographic), E(N)=E(M)+S and N\backslash S=M. Normally, we shall denote such an extension by M+S. 




Let N=M+e be a regular extension of M by an element e . A circuit C of N is said to be an \mathbf{e} -rainbow circuit for (M,c) if e\in C and C-e is a rainbow subset of E(M). We shall often make use of the following elementary observation whose proof we omit.









1.6 Observation




Let (M,c) be a simple, regular, circuit-achromatic coloured matroid where \varepsilon(M)\geq r(M)+1. Then for any regular extension N=M+e by an element e , the there is an e -rainbow circuit C for (M,c) where |C|\leq r(M). 




For a subset X\subseteq E(M) of a matroid M , we say that an element e is a chord of X if e\in\mathrm{cl}(X)-X. For all chords e of X , we refer to the circuits of X+e containing e as \mathbf{X,e} -chordal circuits. Note that for a chord e of a circuit C in a binary matroid M , there are exactly two C,e - chordal circuits.






2 A theorem for circuit-achromatic coloured matroids




Understanding the structure of circuit-achromatic coloured matroids is a necessary aspect of the proof of the main theorem. The primary goal of this section is to provide a theorem for circuit-achromatic regular matroids which shows that such matroids are convenient in the sense that one can find certain pairs of disjoint rainbow circuits in certain regular extensions of these matroids.
To begin with, we shall describe why understanding circuit-achromatic matroids is important.




In the proof of Theorem 1, we use a fairly standard approach for regular matroids which exploits
the well-known decomposition theorem for regular matroids of Seymour [10]. That is, for every regular matroid M , M is either graphic, cographic, isomorphic to R_{10} or M is a 1 -, 2 -, or 3 -sum of two regular matroids. This results in a decomposition of a regular matroid M into 1 -, 2 - and 3 -sums of matroids which are either graphic, cographic or isomorphic to R_{10}. 




Suppose (M,c) is a coloured matroid as described in Theorem 1. The next lemma shows that if M=M_{1}\oplus_{2}M_{2} or M=M_{1}\oplus_{3}M_{2} , then to find a SRC 4 -tuple for (M,c) we may assume that for some i\in[2], M_{i}\backslash E(M_{3-i}) is circuit-achromatic.









2.1 Lemma




Let (M,c) be a simple coloured binary matroid. Suppose there are matroids M_{1} and M_{2} such that for i=1,2, M_{i}\backslash E(M_{3-i}) contains a rainbow circuit and either

i) 



 M=M_{1}\oplus_{2}M_{2} and \varepsilon(M)>r(M)+3 or

ii) 



 M=M_{1}\oplus_{3}M_{2} and for i=1,2, \varepsilon(M\big|E(M_{i}))>r(M_{i})+1. 



Then M has a SRCP.



Proof.



For i=1,2, let M_{i}^{\prime}=M_{i}\backslash E(M_{3-i}) and let C_{i} be a rainbow circuit in M_{i}^{\prime}. 
Suppose that i) holds. Then r(M_{1})+r(M_{2})=r(M)+1. If for some i , |C_{i}|\leq r(M_{i}) , then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq r(M_{1})+r(M_{2})+1=r(M)+2 and \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a SRCP for (M,c), implying that \{C_{1},C_{1},C_{2},C_{2}\} is SRC 4 -tuple. Thus we may assume that for i=1,2 , |C_{i}|=r(M_{i})+1. Then for i=1,2, C_{i} is a spanning circuit for M_{i} . Since \varepsilon(M)>r(M)+3 , there exists i\in[2] such that \varepsilon(M_{i})>r(M_{i})+1 and we may assume that \varepsilon(M_{1})>r(M_{1})+1. Then there exists e\in E(M_{1}^{\prime})-C_{1} and a C_{1},e -chordal circuit C_{1}^{\prime} where C_{1}^{\prime} is a rainbow circuit and |C_{1}^{\prime}|\leq r(M_{1}). Now \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}\} is seen to be a SRCP (and hence (M,c) has a SRC 4 -tuple).




Suppose that ii) holds. Then r(M_{1})+r(M_{2})=r(M)+2 and for i=1,2, \varepsilon(M_{i}^{\prime})>r(M_{i})+1. We claim that for i=1,2, M_{i}^{\prime} has a rainbow circuit C_{i}^{\prime} where |C_{i}^{\prime}|\leq r(M_{i}). If |C_{i}|\leq r(M_{i}) , then we define C_{i}^{\prime}:=C_{i}. 
Suppose instead that |C_{i}|>r(M_{i}). Then |C_{i}|=r(M_{i})+1 and
 C_{i} spans M_{i}^{\prime}. Given that \varepsilon(M_{i}^{\prime})>r(M_{i})+1, there exists e\in E(M_{i}^{\prime})-C_{i} and a C_{i},e -chordal rainbow circuit D for which |D|\leq r(M_{i}). In this case, we define C_{i}^{\prime}:=D. This proves our claim.
We have that |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}^{\prime}|\leq r(M_{1})+r(M_{2})=r(M)+2 and \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is a SRCP (and hence (M,c) has a SRC 4 -tuple).
∎





The main obstacle to proof of Theorem 1 is the case where M is a 3 -sum M=M_{1}\oplus_{3}M_{2} where M_{1}^{\prime}=M_{1}\backslash E(M_{2}) has no rainbow circuits.
The general strategy is to modify M_{2}^{\prime}=M_{2}\backslash E(M_{1}) by adding a subset S^{\prime}\subseteq E(M_{1})\cap E(M_{2}) to M_{2}^{\prime} and colouring these elements so as to obtain a coloured matroid (M_{2}^{\prime\prime},c_{2}^{\prime\prime}) where c_{2}^{\prime\prime} is a 2 -uniform colouring. We will argue inductively, assuming that when M_{2}^{\prime\prime} is simple, (M_{2}^{\prime\prime},c_{2}^{\prime\prime}) contains a SRC 4 -tuple. We will then extend this to a SRC 4 -tuple of (M,c). To do this, we need to find certain pairs of disjoint rainbow circuits in M_{1}. The fact that M_{1}^{\prime} has no rainbow circuits enables one to do this.




Let (M,c) be a simple, regular coloured matroid and let
 N=M+T be a regular extension of M by T. A pairwise-disjoint collection of circuits {\cal C} (resp. cycles) from N is said to be a \mathbf{T} -short rainbow circuit collection (resp. \mathbf{T} -short rainbow cycle collection) for (M,c) if for all \{C,C^{\prime}\}\subset{\binom{{\cal C}}{2}} , |C|+|C^{\prime}|\leq r(M)+2 and for all C\in{\cal C}, |C\cap T|=1 and C-T is a rainbow subset of E(M). 
In the case where {\cal C} is a pair/triple of circuits (resp. cycles), we simply write {\cal C} is a \mathbf{T} - SRCP / \mathbf{T} - SRCT (resp. \mathbf{T} - SRCyP / \mathbf{T} - SRCyT) for (M,c). Note that the existence of a T -SRCyP (resp. T -SRCyT) implies the existence of a T -SRCyP (resp. T -SRCyT) for (M,c). 




A disjoint pair of circuits \{C_{1},C_{2}\} in N is said to be a near- \mathbf{T} -short rainbow circuit pair (near- \mathbf{T} -SRCP) for (M,c) if for i=1,2 , |C_{i}\cap T|=1, C_{i}-T is a rainbow subset and |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq r(M)+3. 
We have the corresponding definition for cycles in place of circuits, where near- \mathbf{T} -SRCyP denotes a near- T -short rainbow cycle pair. The existence of a near- T -SRCyP implies the existence of a near T -SRCP.




Suppose T=\{x\} and {\cal C}=\{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a pair of disjoint circuits (resp. cycles) of N such that C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\{x\}, |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq r(M)+3 and for i=1,2, C_{i}-x is a rainbow subset of E(M). In this case, we say that {\cal C} is \mathbf{x} -semi-short rainbow circuit pair ( \mathbf{x} -semi-SRCP) for (M,c). We have the corresponding definition for cycles where \mathbf{x} -semi-SRCyP denotes an x -near-semi-short rainbow cycle pair.




The next theorem will play a central role in the proof of the main theorem and a large part of this paper is devoted to its proof.









2.2 Theorem




Let M be a rank- n, simple regular matroid where 2n-2\leq\varepsilon(M)\leq 2n-1 and let c be a 2 -bounded n -colouring of M where (M,c) is circuit-achromatic
Let N=M+T be a regular extension of M where T is a co-independent 3 -circuit in N. If \varepsilon(M)=2n-1, then let e be the unique colour-singular element in (M,c). 

i) 



Suppose \varepsilon(M)=2n-1. Then either

i.1) 



there exists a T -SRCT for (M,c) 
or

i.2) 



for all x\in T, there exists a T -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} for (M-e,c) where x\not\in C_{1}\cup C_{2}. 



ii) 



Suppose \varepsilon(M)=2n-1 and M+x is a regular extension of M by an element x. If x and e are non-parallel, then there exists an x -semi-SRCP for (M,c). 

iii) 



Suppose \varepsilon(M)=2n-2. Then there exists a T -SRCP for (M,c). 






An important tool used in the proof of the above theorem is a structure theorem for circuit-achromatic binary matroids which will be given in the next section.





3 Colourings of Binary Matroids




As outlined in the previous section, the proof of Theorem 1 depends largely on Theorem 2. In this section, we shall describe a structure theorem for circuit-achromatic binary matroids which will be instrumental in the proof of this theorem.




Let M be a rank- n binary matroid. Let c:E(M)\rightarrow[k] be a k -colouring of M having colour classes X_{1},\dots,X_{k} . The colouring c is said to be stratified if there exists a permutation i_{1},\dots,i_{k} of [k] such that
for j=1,\dots,k, S_{j}=X_{i_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup X_{i_{j}} is closed (i.e. S_{j}=\mathrm{cl}_{M}(S_{j}) ) and 1\leq r(S_{1})<r(S_{2})<\cdots<r(S_{k})=n. We call X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}},\dots,X_{i_{k}} a stratification of c. We claim that if c is a stratified colouring, then (M,c) is circuit-achromatic. For suppose X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}},\dots,X_{i_{k}} is a stratification of c. Let C be a circuit of M and let j^{*} be the greatest integer j for which C\cap X_{i_{j}}\neq\mathchar 31. Then clearly |C\cap X_{i_{j^{*}}}|\geq 2 and hence C is not a rainbow circuit.
In [3] it was shown that the converse holds if c is an r(M) - colouring of a binary matroid M for which (M,c) is circuit-achromatic.









3.1 Theorem ( [3] )




Let M be a binary matroid and let c be an r(M) -colouring of M . Then (M,c) is circuit-achromatic if and only if c is stratified.




The above theorem has the following useful corollaries.









3.2 Corollary




Let be a rank- n binary matroid M having an n -colouring c with colour classes X_{1}.\dots,X_{n} . If (M,c) is circuit - achromatic, then

i) 



for some i , the elements of X_{i} form a parallel class of M , and

ii) 



for some i , X_{i} is a cocircuit of M. 





Proof.



By Theorem 1, c is stratified and hence there exists a permutation i_{1},\dots,i_{n} of [n] such that
for j=1,\dots,n, S_{j}=X_{i_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup X_{i_{j}} is closed and 1\leq r(S_{1})<r(S_{2})<\cdots<r(S_{n})=n. It follows that for all j\in[n], r(S_{j})=j . In particular,
 r(X_{i_{1}})=r(S_{1})=1 hence X_{i_{1}} is a parallel class. Furthermore, since r(S_{n-1})=n-1 and S_{n-1} is closed, it follows that
 X_{i_{n}} is a cocircuit.
∎





The next corollary is an immediate consequence of the above.









3.3 Corollary




If M is a simple matroid with an r(M) -colouring c for which (M,c) has no colour-singular elements, then (M,c) contains a rainbow circuit.





4 Proof for graphic and cographic matroids




Our first task is to prove Theorem 1 for graphic and cograph matroids. In this section, we shall prove the following theorem:









4.1 Theorem




Let (M,c) be simple coloured matroid where c is a 2 -uniform (r(M)+1) -colouring
and M is either graphic or cographic. Then M has a SRC 4 -tuple.




The proof of this theorem builds on some of the ideas in the proof of [6, Theorem 5].




Let M be a matroid. We define a theta-subset in M to be a subset \Theta\subseteq E(M) for which \Theta contains exactly three circuits C_{1},C_{2},C_{3} where for all permutations i,j,k of [3] C_{i}\triangle C_{j}=C_{k}. A disjoint pair (\Theta,C) where \Theta is a rainbow theta-subset and C is a rainbow circuit is said to be a rainbow theta-circuit pair (RThCP).




For a RThCP (\Theta,C) ) we define \psi(\Theta,C):=\left\lceil\frac{|C|}{2}\right\rceil+|\Theta| .
A short rainbow theta-circuit pair (SRThCP) is a RThCP (\Theta,C) where \psi(\Theta,C)\leq r(M)+3. 




4.1 A lemma for graphic matroids




We shall first prove the following lemma:









4.2 Lemma




Let M be a simple graphic matroid and let c be a 2 -uniform (r(M)+1) -colouring for M. 
Then either

i) 



 (M,c) has a SRCP or

ii) 



 (M,c) has a RThCP (\Theta,C) where \psi(\Theta,C)\leq r(M)+2 , or

iii) 



 (M,c) has a SRThCP (\Theta,C) where \Theta has at most two chords and for all chords e of \Theta and all \Theta,e -chordal circuits D_{e} , \Theta-D_{e} is independent. Furthermore, either

iii.1) 



 C contains one chord of \Theta, |C| is odd, and 2|\Theta|+|C|\leq 2r(M)+5, or

iii.2) 



 C contains two chords of \Theta, |C| is even and 2|\Theta|+|C|\leq 2r(M)+6. 







Proof.



Let M=M(G). The proof is by induction on \nu(G) . It suffices to prove the lemma when G is connected. Since M is simple, {\binom{\nu(G)}{2}}\geq\varepsilon(G)=\varepsilon(M)=2(r(M)+1)=2\nu(G) and thus \nu(G)\geq 5. 
When \nu(G)=5 , we have G\simeq K_{5} and it can be shown that (M,c) has a SRCP. Suppose that n>5 and the lemma holds for graphs with fewer than n vertices.
Let (G,c) be the coloured graph associated with (M,c) where \nu(G)=n. 
Suppose first that G contains a rainbow vertex v. Let G^{\prime}=G-v . Then |c(G^{\prime})|=n=\nu(G^{\prime})+1. 
Thus there is a component K of G^{\prime} for which |c(K)|\geq\nu(K)+1. Let T be a rainbow spanning tree of K (which has \nu(K)-1 edges) and let \{f_{1},f_{2}\}\subset E(K)-E(T) where c\left(\{f_{1},f_{2}\}\right)\cap c(T)=\mathchar 31 ; such edges exist since |c(K)-c(T)|\geq 2. For i=1,2, there is a unique (rainbow) cycle C_{i} in T+f_{i}. 
If |V(C_{1})\cap V(C_{2})|\leq 1, then C_{1},C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles for which |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n+1 and hence \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a SRCP and i) holds. If |V(C_{1})\cap V(C_{2})|\geq 2, then the subgraph of T+\{f_{1},f_{2}\} induced by E(C_{1})\cup E(C_{2}) contains a rainbow theta subgraph. In this case, G contains a rainbow subgraph \overline{\Theta} and we may assume that among such subgraphs, \varepsilon(\overline{\Theta}) is minimum. Let \Theta=E(\overline{\Theta}) and let D_{i},\ i=1,2,3 be the circuits of \Theta. Given that |c(G-\Theta)|=n, it follows that G-\Theta contains a rainbow cycle, say \overline{C} . Let C=E(\overline{C}). 
Suppose e is a chord of \Theta and D_{e} is a \Theta,e -chordal circuit. Suppose that for some i , D_{i}\subseteq\Theta-D_{e}. If D_{e} is not a rainbow circuit, then \Theta^{\prime}=\Theta\triangle D_{e} is seen to be a rainbow theta-subset of M where \varepsilon(\Theta^{\prime})<\varepsilon(\Theta), contradicting the minimality of \Theta. Thus D_{e} is a rainbow circuit and
 \{D_{e},D_{i}\} is seen to be a SRCP and ii) holds. Because of this,
we may assume that for all chords e of \Theta and for all
 \Theta,e -chordal circuits D_{e} , \Theta-D_{e} is independent.
By this assumption and the minimality of \Theta , it can be shown that at most two elements of M are chords of \Theta. In particular, at most two elements of C are chords of \Theta. 
If no elements of C are chords of \Theta, then |V(\overline{C})\cap(V(G)-V(\overline{\Theta})|\geq\left\lceil\frac{|C|}{2}\right\rceil and hence \nu(\overline{\Theta})+\left\lceil\frac{|C|}{2}\right\rceil\leq n implying that
 |\Theta|+\left\lceil\frac{|C|}{2}\right\rceil\leq n+1=r(M)+2 and \psi(\Theta,C)\leq r(M)+2. In this case, ii) holds. Suppose one element of C is a chord of \Theta. Then |V(\overline{C})\cap(V(G)-V(\Theta))|\geq\left\lceil\frac{|C|-1}{2}\right\rceil and hence
 |\Theta|+\left\lceil\frac{|C|-1}{2}\right\rceil\leq n+1=r(M)+2. If |C| is even then \left\lceil\frac{|C|-1}{2}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{|C|}{2}\right\rceil in which case |\Theta|+\left\lceil\frac{|C|}{2}\right\rceil\leq r(M)+2 and \psi(\Theta,C)\leq r(M)+2 and ii) holds again.
If |C| is odd, then \left\lceil\frac{|C|-1}{2}\right\rceil=\frac{|C|-1}{2} and 2|\Theta|+|C|\leq 2(n+1)+1=2r(M)+5. In this case, iii.1) holds. If two elements of C are chords of \Theta, then |V(\overline{C})\cap(V(G)-V(\Theta)|\geq\left\lceil\frac{|C|-2}{2}\right\rceil and hence
 |\Theta|+\left\lceil\frac{|C|}{2}\right\rceil\leq n+2=r(M)+3. As such, we have that 2|\Theta|+|C|\leq 2(n+2)=2r(M)+6 and iii.2) holds.




From the above, we may assume that G has no rainbow vertices. In particular, this implies that for all v , d_{G}(v)\geq 2. Suppose that for some vertex v, d_{G}(v)=2. Let E_{G}(v)=\{e,f\} . Since v is not a rainbow vertex, c(e)=c(f). Let G^{\prime}=G/e\backslash f and c^{\prime}=c\big|E(G^{\prime}). 
Since G is simple, G^{\prime} is seen to be simple and |c(G^{\prime})|=2\varepsilon(G^{\prime}). By assumption, the lemma holds for M^{\prime}=M(G^{\prime}) and using this, one can show that the lemma holds for M as well.
Thus we may assume that for all v\in V(G), d_{G}(v)\geq 3. Suppose there is a vertex v for which d_{G}(v)=3. Let E_{G}(v)=\{e,f,g\}. Since v is not a rainbow vertex, we may assume that c(e)=c(f). Let G^{\prime}=G/e\backslash f and c^{\prime}=c\big|E(G^{\prime}). Then G^{\prime} is simple and |c^{\prime}(G^{\prime})|=2\varepsilon(G^{\prime}) . By assumption, the theorem holds for M^{\prime}=M(G^{\prime}). Suppose that i) holds for M^{\prime}. Then G^{\prime} contains a SRCP say \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\}. If neither of these cycles contains g , then \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is a SRCP for (G,c). Suppose that one the cycles contains g, say C_{1}^{\prime}. Then the cycle C_{1} in G where E(C_{1})=E(C_{1}^{\prime})+e is a rainbow cycle. Letting C_{2}=C_{2}^{\prime}, we see that \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a SRCP for (G,c). Suppose that ii) holds M^{\prime}. Then (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) has RThCP (\Theta^{\prime},C^{\prime}) where \psi(\Theta,C^{\prime})\leq r(M^{\prime})+2=r(M)+1. 
We may assume that g\in C^{\prime}\cup\Theta^{\prime} , for otherwise, (\Theta^{\prime},C^{\prime}) is a RThCP for M , and ii) holds for M. Suppose g\in C^{\prime}. Then there is a rainbow cycle C in G where E(C)=E(C^{\prime})+e. Then (\Theta^{\prime},C) is seen to be a RThCP in M 
where \psi(\Theta^{\prime},C)\leq\psi(\Theta^{\prime},C^{\prime})+1\leq r(M)+2 and ii) holds for M. On the other hand, suppose g\in\Theta^{\prime}. Then there is a rainbow theta-subgraph \Theta of G where E(\Theta)=E(\Theta^{\prime})+e. Then (\Theta,C^{\prime}) is seen to be a RThCP in M 
where \psi(\Theta,C^{\prime})=\psi(\Theta^{\prime},C^{\prime})+1\leq r(M)+2 and ii) holds for M. Lastly, it is straightforward to show that if iii) holds for M^{\prime} , then either i), ii), or iii) holds for M. 




From the above, we may assume that for all vertices v , d_{G}(v)\geq 4. Given that 2\varepsilon(G)=4n, its average degree is 4 and thus G must be 4 -regular. Furthermore, since G is simple and has no rainbow vertices, each vertex v has exactly two incident edges of the same colour. We shall direct the edges of G as follows: for all vertices v , if e,f\in E_{G}(v) and c(e)=c(f), then both e and f will be directed away from v. The resulting directed graph \overrightarrow{G} is 2 -regular; that is, the in-degree and out-degree of each vertex equals two. Using some of the ideas from [2], we shall show that i) holds. We first observe that every directed cycle of \overrightarrow{G} corresponds to a rainbow cycle in G. Suppose \overrightarrow{G} contains four edge-disjoint directed cycles, say C_{i},i\in[4] . Given that \sum_{i}\varepsilon(C_{i})\leq\varepsilon=2n, either \varepsilon(C_{1})+\varepsilon(C_{2})\leq n, or \varepsilon(C_{3})+\varepsilon(C_{4})\leq n, implying that either \{C_{1},C_{2}\} or \{C_{3},C_{4}\} is a SRCP. Thus we may assume that \overrightarrow{G} has at most 3 edge-disjoint directed cycles.




For each vertex, one can find two edge-disjoint directed cycles which contain the vertex. Among all vertices and such pairs of directed cycles, let v be a vertex and C_{1},C_{2} be edge-disjoint directed cycles such that |C_{1}|+|C_{2}| is minimum. Let \overrightarrow{H} be the directed subgraph induced by C_{1}\cup C_{2}. We first observe that for all vertices u\in V(\overrightarrow{H}) where d_{H}(u)=4, \overrightarrow{H}-u contains no directed cycle; if \overrightarrow{H}-u did contain a directed cycle \overrightarrow{C} then \overrightarrow{H}^{\prime}=\overrightarrow{H}-E(\overrightarrow{C}) is Eulerian (in-degree = out-degree for all vertices) and given that u has degree 4 in \overrightarrow{H}^{\prime} , it follows that \overrightarrow{H}^{\prime} has two edge-disjoint directed cycles C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime} containing u , contradicting the minimality of C_{1},C_{2}. 
Since \overrightarrow{G} has at most three edge-disjoint directed cycles, it follow that V(\overrightarrow{H})=V(\overrightarrow{G}). If V(C_{1})\cap V(C_{2})=\{v\}, then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n+1=r(M)+2 and \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a SRCP. Thus we may assume that there exists u\in V(C_{1})\cap V(C_{2})-v. We claim that we may assume that there is no edge e\in E(G)-E(H) which is a chord of C_{1} or C_{2} . For suppose e is a chord of C_{1}. Let C_{1j},\ j=1,2 be the C_{1},e -chordal cycles.
Suppose first that u\in V(C_{11}) and v\in V(C_{12}). Seeing as one of C_{11} or C_{12} is a directed cycle in \overrightarrow{G}, either u together with C_{11},C_{2} or v together with C_{12},C_{2} contradicts the minimality of v and C_{1},C_{2}. On the other hand, suppose that \{u,v\}\subset V(C_{11}). 
If C_{11} is a directed cycle in \overrightarrow{G}, then v together with C_{11},C_{2} contradicts the minimality of v and C_{1},C_{2}. Thus C_{12} is a directed circuit. If V(C_{12})\cap V(C_{2})=\mathchar 31, then |C_{12}|+|C_{2}|\leq n and \{C_{12},C_{2}\} is is a SRCP. If there exists
 v^{\prime}\in V(C_{12})\cap V(C_{2}), then v^{\prime} together with C_{12},C_{2} contradicts the minimality of v,C_{2},C_{2}. Thus we may assume that no edge of E(G)-E(H) is a chord of C_{1} or C_{2}. 




Given that \overrightarrow{H}-v has no directed cycles, we can describe \overrightarrow{H} as follows: For i=1,2 , C_{i} decomposes into directed paths P_{i1},P_{i2}.\dots,P_{ik} where for j=1,\dots,k P_{ij} is directed from u_{i} to u_{i+1}, where u_{1}=u_{k+1}=v and V(C_{1})\cap V(C_{2})=\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}. Since G is simple, for all i , V(P_{i1}\cup P_{i2})-\{u_{i},u_{i+1}\}\neq\mathchar 31. Since \overrightarrow{G} has at most 3 edge-disjoint directed cycles, all the vertices of V(G)-\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\} belong to one directed cycle \overrightarrow{C}. Thus for some i_{1}<i_{2}, and
 j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,2\} there is an edge e\in E(\overrightarrow{C}) directed from v_{i_{1}} in P_{i_{1}j_{1}}-u_{i_{1}}-u_{i_{1}+1} to v_{i_{2}} in P_{i_{2}j_{2}}-u_{i_{2}}-u_{i_{2}+1}. By assumption, we have that j_{1}\neq j_{2}. We may change \overrightarrow{C}_{1} and \overrightarrow{C}_{2} into directed cycles \overrightarrow{C}_{1}^{\prime} and \overrightarrow{C}_{2}^{\prime} by swapping the paths P_{i_{1}1} and P_{i_{1}2}. It now follows that e is a chord of \overrightarrow{C}_{1}^{\prime} or \overrightarrow{C}_{2}^{\prime} . Using C_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2 in place of C_{1},C_{2} , we can argue as before. This completes the proof.
∎





4.1.1 The proof of Theorem 4 for graphic matroids




Let (M,c) be a simple coloured matroid where c is a 2 -uniform (r(M)+1) -colouring
and M is graphic. Let M=M(G) where \nu(G)=n. Suppose (M,c) has a SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} . Then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq r(M)+2 and \{C_{1},C_{1},C_{2},C_{2}\} is seen to be a SRC 4 -tuple. Thus we may assume that (M,c) has no SRCP. Suppose (M,c) has a RThCP (\Theta,C) where \psi(\Theta,C)\leq r(M)+2. Then
 2|\Theta|+|C|\leq 2r(M)+4. Let C_{i},\ i=1,2,3 be the circuits of \Theta. Then \sum_{i}|C_{i}|=2|\Theta| and hence \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C\} is seen to be a SRC 4 -tuple. Thus we may assume that no such RThCP exists.
Then Lemma 4.1 iii) holds, and there exists a SRThCP (\Theta,C) where \Theta has at most two chords and for all chords e of \Theta and all \Theta,e -chordal circuits D_{e}, \Theta-D_{e} is independent.




Let C_{i},\ i=1,2,3 be the circuits of \Theta. Suppose that Lemma 4.1 iii.1) holds.
Let e_{1} the edge of C which is a chord of \Theta. Then for some i , e is a chord of C_{i} and we may assume this is true for i=1. Let C_{1j},\ j=1,2 be the C_{1},e -chordal cycles.
At least one of these is a rainbow circuit, say C_{11}. Now C_{11},C_{2},C_{3},C are rainbow circuits for which no element of M belongs to more than two of the circuits. Since |C_{11}|+|C_{12}|=|C_{1}|+2, it follows that




 \displaystyle|C_{11}|+|C_{2}|+|C_{3}|+|C| 
 \displaystyle=|C_{1}|+2-|C_{12}|+|C_{2}|+|C_{3}|+|C| 




 \displaystyle\leq 2|\Theta|+|C|+(2-|C_{12}|) 




 \displaystyle\leq 2r(M)+5-1=2r(M)+4. 



Thus \{C_{11},C_{2},C_{3},C\} is a SRC 4 -tuple.
Suppose instead that Lemma 4.1 iii.2) holds. Let e_{i},\ i=1,2 be the elements of C which are chords of \Theta. Suppose first that there exists a 2 -subset \{i_{1},i_{2}\}\subset[3] such that for j=1,2, e_{j} is a chord of C_{i_{j}}. 
For convenience, we may assume i_{j}=j,\ j=1,2. For j=1,2 , there is an C_{j},e_{j} -chordal circuit D_{j} which is a rainbow circuit. We see that




 \displaystyle|D_{1}|+|D_{2}|+|C_{3}|+|C| 
 \displaystyle\leq|C_{1}|-1+|C_{2}|-1+|C_{3}|+|C| 




 \displaystyle=2|\Theta|+|C|-2\leq 2r(M)+4. 



Thus \{D_{1},D_{2},C_{3},C\} is seen to be a SRC 4 -tuple. Suppose instead that for some i\in[3], e_{1} and e_{2} are chords of C_{i}. We may assume that e_{i},\ i=1,2 are chords of C_{1}. 
For i=1,2 , there is an C_{1},e_{i} -chordal circuit D_{i} which is a rainbow circuit. Since for i=1,2 , \Theta-D_{i} contains no circuits, it can be shown that at least one of \{D_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C\}, \{D_{2},C_{2},C_{3},C\} or \{D_{1}\triangle D_{2},C_{2},C_{3},C\} is a SRC 4 -tuple.
This completes the proof.






4.2 The proof of Theorem 4 when M is cographic




Let (M,c) be a simple coloured matroid where c is a 2 -uniform (r(M)+1) -colouring
and M is cographic.
Let M=M^{*}(G) where G is a connected graph with n vertices and having no 2-cocycles.
Then \varepsilon(M)=2(r(M)+1)=2(\varepsilon(G)-n+2). Since \varepsilon(G)=\varepsilon(M), it follows that \varepsilon(M)=\varepsilon(G)=2(n-2) and r(M)=n-3. 
Let (G,c) be the coloured graph inherited from (M,c). Then c is a 2 -uniform (n-2) -colouring.
It will suffice show that (M,c) has a SRCP; that is, (G,c) has two edge-disjoint rainbow cocycles C_{1} and C_{2} such that |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n-1. We shall proceed by induction on n. The smallest graph G in question has 6 vertices and the theorem is seen to be true in this case.
Assume that for graphs with fewer than n vertices there is a SRCP.
Suppose G contains a monochromatic digon \{e,f\} . Let G^{\prime}=G/e\backslash f and c^{\prime}=c\big|E(G^{\prime}). Then G^{\prime} is connected and has no 2 -cocycles (since a cocycle of G^{\prime} is seen to be a cocycle of G ). By assumption, G^{\prime} has disjoint rainbow cocycles C_{1},C_{2} where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n-2 and these are also seen to be cocycles in G. Therefore we may assume that G^{\prime} has no monochromatic digons. Since |c(G)|=n-2, there are two rainbow vertices u_{1},u_{2} in G . Suppose that u_{1},u_{2} are non-adjacent.
Let C_{j}=E_{G}(u_{j}),\ j=1,2. If |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n-1, then \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a SRCP. Suppose |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\geq n. Then \sum_{v\in V(G)-\{u_{1},u_{2}\}}d_{G}(v)\leq 4(n-2)-n=3(n-2)-2. Thus there exists a vertex u_{3}\in V(G)-\{u_{1},u_{2}\} where d_{G}(u_{3})\leq 2. Given that G has no vertices of degree 2 , it follows that d_{G}(u_{3})=1. Let e=u_{3}v and let \{e,e^{\prime}\} be the colour class containing e. Let G^{\prime}=G/e\backslash e^{\prime} and let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(G^{\prime}) . By assumption, (G^{\prime},c^{\prime}) contains two edge-disjoint rainbow cocycles D_{1} and D_{2} where |D_{1}|+|D_{2}|\leq n-2. Now either D_{1} or D_{1}+e^{\prime} is a rainbow cocycle of G and we let D_{1}^{\prime} be one which is. Furthermore, D_{2}^{\prime}=E_{G}(u_{3})=\{e\} is a rainbow cocycle of G and
 |D_{1}^{\prime}|+|D_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|D_{1}|+2\leq n-1. Thus \{D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime}\} is a SRCP for (M,c). 




From the above, we may assume that any two rainbow vertices are adjacent. Let u_{i},\ i\in[k] be all the rainbow vertices in G . By the above, we may assume that each pair of vertices is adjacent. However, this implies that for each edge e_{ij}=u_{i}u_{j} , no two edges with colour c(e_{ij}) are incident. Thus there are at least n-((n-2)-\binom{k}{2})>k rainbow vertices. This yields a contradiction.






5 Circuit-achromatic graphic matroids M with
 2r(M)-1 elements




Towards proving Theorem 2, we shall first examine the case where M=M(G) is a simple graphic matroid and \varepsilon(M)=2r(M)-1. 
For the remainder of this section, we let M=M(G) be a simple graphic matroid where G is connected, n=\nu(G)\geq 3, and \varepsilon(G)=2(n-1)-1=2n-3. Let c:E(G)\rightarrow[n-1] be an (n-1) -colouring of M where |c^{-1}(1)|=1 and |c^{-1}(i)|=2,\ i=2,\dots,n-1. 
For i=1,\dots,n-1 let X_{i}=c^{-1}(i) where X_{1}=\{e_{1}\} and for all i\geq 2, X_{i}=\{e_{i1},e_{i2}\}. 
Assume that (M,c) is circuit - achromatic. It follows by Theorem 3 that the colouring c is stratified and we may assume that the graph G is constructed as follows:
Let G_{1} be the graph consisting of a single edge e_{1}=v_{11}v_{12}. Next let G_{2} be the graph obtained from G_{1} by adding the vertex v_{2} and edges e_{21}=v_{2}v_{21} and e_{22}=v_{2}v_{22}. Continuing, assume that we have constructed G_{i-1}. We construct G_{i} by adding to G_{i-1} the vertex v_{i} and edges e_{i1}=v_{i}v_{i1} and e_{i2}=v_{i}v_{i2} where \{v_{i1},v_{i2}\}\subseteq V(G_{i-1}). The construction finishes with G=G_{n-1}. Note that by our construction, for all 2\leq i\leq n-1, the minimum vertex degree in G_{i} is 2 and \nu(G_{i})=i+1. 









5.1 Lemma




For all pairs of distinct vertices \{u,v\}\subseteq G , dist_{G}^{c}(u,v)\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor. Furthermore, when n is even, there is at most one pair of vertices u,v for which dist_{G}^{c}(u,v)=\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor=\frac{n}{2}. 



Proof.



We shall prove the assertion is true for G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{n-1}=G by induction. That is, we shall show that for all i , for all pairs of distinct vertices \{u,v\}\subseteq G_{i} , dist_{G_{i}}^{c}(u,v)\leq\lfloor\frac{\nu(G_{i})}{2}\rfloor=\lfloor\frac{i+1}{2}\rfloor, and when i is odd, there is only one pair of vertices for which equality holds.
The assertion is clearly true for G_{1} . Suppose that for i=1,\dots,k-1 , the lemma is true for G_{i} . We shall prove that the assertion is true for G_{k}. 
We have e_{k1}=v_{k}v_{k1} and e_{k2}=v_{k}v_{k2} . Let u,v be a distinct pair of vertices in V(G_{k}). If \{u,v\}\subseteq V(G_{k-1}), then by assumption, dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v)\leq\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor\leq\lfloor\frac{k+1}{2}\rfloor. 
Thus we may assume that v=v_{k}. 




Suppose k is odd.
Let k=2\ell-1. By assumption, for all j\in\{1,2\} and for all u\in V(G_{k-1})-v_{kj}, dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(v_{kj},u)\leq\ell-1. 
Suppose there exists a distinct pair of vertices \{u,u^{\prime}\}\subseteq V(G_{k-1})-\{v_{k1},v_{k2}\} such that for j=1,2 , dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v_{kj})=d_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u^{\prime},v_{kj})=\ell-1. We observe that at least three of the vertices in \{u,u^{\prime},v_{k1},v_{k2}\} , say x,y,z, belong to V(G_{k-2}). Since k-2 is odd, it follows by assumption that for at most one pair of vertices in \{x,y,z\} , the rainbow distance between these two vertices is at most \ell-1. However, this is contradicted by the assumption that for j=1,2 , dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v_{kj})=dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u^{\prime},v_{kj})=\ell-1. Thus there is at most one vertex u\in V(G_{k-1})-\{v_{k1},v_{k2}\} for which dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v_{k1})=dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v_{k2})=\ell-1. It now follows that for all u\in V(G_{k})-v , dist_{G_{k}}^{c}(u,v)\leq\ell, and there is at most one vertex u for which equality holds.




Suppose k is even.
Let k=2\ell. If u\in\{v_{k1},v_{k2}\} , then dist_{G_{k}}^{c}(u,v)=1\leq\ell. Thus we may assume that u\not\in\{v_{k1},v_{k2}\}. 
Given that \{u,v_{k1},v_{k2}\}\subseteq V(G_{k-1}) and \nu(G_{k-1})=k=2\ell , which is even, it follows that for j=1,2, dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v_{kj})\leq\ell and the inequality is strict for at least one j\in\{1,2\}. We may assume that dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v_{k1})<\ell. 
Then dist_{G_{k}}^{c}(u,v)\leq dist_{G_{k-1}}^{c}(u,v_{k1})+1\leq\ell. 
This completes the proof.
∎










5.2 Lemma




Let u_{1},u_{2},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime} be vertices in G where the vertices are distinct except for possibly u_{1}=u_{2}. If u=u_{1}=u_{2}, then there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i},\ i=1,2 in G from u to u_{i}^{\prime} where |P_{i}|+|P_{2}|\leq n-1. If u_{1}\neq u_{2}, then there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i},\ i=1,2 in G originating at distinct vertices in \{u_{1},u_{2}\} and terminating at distinct vertices in \{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\} where |P_{i}|+|P_{2}|\leq n-2. 



Proof.



We shall prove that the lemma is true for G_{i},\ i=2,3,\dots,n-1 using induction on i. One can easily check that the lemma is true when i\in\{2,3\}. Suppose that for all i<k the lemma is true for G_{i} where k\geq 4. We will show that it is true for G_{k}. Let u_{1},u_{2},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\in V(G_{k}) where the vertices are distinct except for possibly u_{1}=u_{2}. Suppose first that u=u_{1}=u_{2}. If \{u,u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\}\subseteq V(G_{k-1}), then by assumption, there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{1},P_{2} in G_{k-1} where for j=1,2, P_{j} has terminal vertices u to u_{j}^{\prime} and |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{k-1})-1=k-1. Thus we may assume that v_{k}\in\{u,u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\}. 




Suppose u=v_{k}. Recall that e_{kj}=v_{k}v_{kj},\ j=1,2. Assume first that \{v_{k1},v_{k2}\}\cap\{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\}=\mathchar 31. By assumption, there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2 in G_{k-1} originating at
distinct vertices in \{v_{k1},v_{k2}\} and terminating at distinct vertices in \{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\} where |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(G_{k-1})-2=k-2. We may assume that v_{kj}\in V(P_{j}^{\prime}),\ j=1,2. For j=1,2, let P_{j}=P_{j}^{\prime}+e_{kj}+u. 
Then P_{j},\ j=1,2 are edge-disjoint rainbow paths from u to vertices in \{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\} where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq k=\nu(G_{k})-1. 
Suppose now that \{v_{k1},v_{k2}\}\cap\{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\}\neq\mathchar 31. We may assume that
 v_{k1}=u_{1}^{\prime}. Suppose v_{k2}\neq u_{2}^{\prime}. 
By Lemma 5, there is a rainbow path P_{2}^{\prime} from v_{k2} to u_{2}^{\prime} in G_{k-1} having length at most \lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor. Let P_{1}=uu_{1}^{\prime} and P_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}+e_{k2}+u. Then |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq 1+\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor\leq k-1 (since k\geq 4 ).
Suppose that v_{k2}=u_{2}^{\prime}. Then the paths P_{i}=uu_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2 will suffice.




From the above, we may assume that u\neq v_{k} and thus either v_{k}=u_{1}^{\prime} or v_{k}=u_{2}^{\prime}. Without loss of generality, we may assume that v_{k}=u_{1}^{\prime}. 
Furthermore, we may assume that v_{k1}\neq u_{2}^{\prime}. By assumption there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2 in G_{k-1} from u to the vertices v_{k1} and u_{2}^{\prime} where |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(G_{k-1})-1=k-1. Let P_{1}=P_{1}^{\prime}+e_{k1}+u_{1}^{\prime} and let P_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}. Then we have that |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq k=\nu(G_{k})-1. This proves the lemma in the case where u_{1}=u_{2}. 




From the above, we may assume that u_{1}\neq u_{2}. As before, we may assume that v_{k}\in\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\} . Without loss of generality, we may assume that v_{k}=u_{1}. Suppose first that v_{k1}\in\{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\} . We may assume that v_{k1}=u_{1}^{\prime}. Let P_{1}=u_{1}u_{1}^{\prime} . By Lemma 5, there is a rainbow path P_{2} in G_{k-1} from u_{2} to u_{2}^{\prime} of length at most \lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor .
Now |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor+1\leq\frac{k}{2}+1\leq k-1=\nu(G_{k})-2 . The last inequality follows from the fact that k\geq 4. Thus we may assume that v_{k1}\not\in\{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\} and by symmetry, the same holds for v_{k2}. 
Either u_{2}\neq v_{k1} or u_{2}\neq v_{k2}, and we may assume that u_{2}\neq v_{k1}. By assumption, there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2 in G_{k-1} originating at vertices in \{v_{k1},u_{2}\} and terminating at vertices in \{u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\} where |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(G_{k-1})-2=k-2. We may assume that v_{k1}\in V(P_{1}^{\prime}). Let P_{1}=P_{1}^{\prime}+e_{k1}+u_{1} and let P_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}. Then |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq k-1=\nu(G_{k})-2. This completes the proof.
∎










5.3 Lemma




Let u,v\in V(G) where \{u,v\}\neq\{v_{11},v_{22}\}. Then there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i},\ i=1,2 from u to v where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq n. 



Proof.



By induction, we shall prove that the assertion is true for all G_{i},\ i\in[n-1]. That is, for all i\in[n-1], we shall show that if u,v\in V(G_{i}) where \{u,v\}\neq\{v_{11},v_{22}\}, then there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{j},\ j=1,2 in G_{i} from u to v where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{i})=i+1. 
The assertion is clearly true for G_{1} and G_{2}. 
Assume that the lemma holds for all G_{i} , where i<k and k\geq 4. If \{u,v\}\subseteq V(G_{k-1}), then by assumption there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i},\ i=1,2 in G_{k-1} from u to v where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{k-1})=k. Thus we may assume that u=v_{k}. Suppose first that v\not\in\{v_{k1},v_{k2}\} .
Given that k\geq 4, it follows by Lemma 5 that there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{j}^{\prime},\ j=1,2 from v to v_{kj} where |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(G_{k-1})-1=k-1. For j=1,2, let P_{j}=P_{j}^{\prime}+e_{kj}+u . Then P_{i},\ i=1,2 are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths from u to v where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq k+1=\nu(G_{k}). Suppose instead that v\in\{v_{k1},v_{k2}\}. Here we may assume that v=v_{k1}. Let P_{1}=uv_{k1}. By Lemma 5, there is a rainbow path P_{2}^{\prime} in G_{k-1} from v_{k2} to v where |P_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor. Let P_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}+e_{k2}+u . Then P_{i},\ i=1,2 are edge-disjoint rainbow paths from u to v where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor+2\leq\frac{k}{2}+2\leq k+1. Thus the assertion is true for G_{k} . The proof now follows by induction.
∎





Let K_{23}^{+} denote the graph obtained from the complete bipartite graph K_{23} by adding an edge between the two vertices of degree three.









5.4 Lemma




Assume that G\not\simeq K_{23}^{+}. Let N=M+T be a graphic extension of M where T is a 3 -circuit of N. Then there exists an T -SRCT for (M,c). 



Proof.



We first note that without the assumption that G\not\simeq K_{23}^{+} the theorem is false. We shall use a proof by induction on n. The lemma is seen to be true for the smallest case n=3. Assume that n\geq 4 and the assertion is true for graphs having fewer than n vertices. We shall show that the assertion holds for G as well. Let T=\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\} and H=G+T where in H , f_{1}=u_{1}u_{2},\ f_{2}=u_{1}u_{3}, and f_{3}=u_{2}u_{3}. Let U=\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}. 









(A) We may assume that if u is a vertex of degree two in G , then u\in U. 



Proof.



Suppose G has a vertex u\not\in\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\} of degree two where E_{G}(u)=\{e,f\}. If c(e)=c(f), then let G^{\prime}=G/e\backslash f and M^{\prime}=M/e\backslash f. Let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(M^{\prime}). If G^{\prime}\simeq K_{23}^{+}, then it is easy to find a T -SRCT for (M,c). Thus we may assume that G^{\prime}\not\simeq K_{23}^{+} and hence G^{\prime} satisfies the conditions of the lemma and hence there is a T -SRCT for (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) which is also seen to be a T -SRCT for G^{\prime}. Suppose c(e)\neq c(f). Then u\in\{v_{11},v_{12}\} and e_{1}\in\{e,f\}. Furthermore, assuming that e=e_{1} we have that f\in X_{2} and we may assume that f=e_{21}. 
Now let G^{\prime}=G/e_{21}\backslash e_{22}, M^{\prime}=M/e_{21}\backslash e_{22}, and c^{\prime}=c\big|E(M^{\prime}). Then as before, regardless of whether G^{\prime}\simeq K_{23}^{+} or not, (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) has a T -SRCT \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} . If e_{1}\not\in C_{1}^{\prime}\cup C_{2}^{\prime}\cup C_{3}^{\prime} , then \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). If e_{1}\in C_{1}^{\prime}\cup C_{2}^{\prime}\cup C_{3}^{\prime} , and say e_{1}\in C_{1}^{\prime} , then there is a circuit C_{1} where E(C_{1})=E(C_{1}^{\prime})+e_{21} and \{C_{1},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
∎










(B) We may assume that there is no vertex v\in V(G)-U where d_{G}(v)=3 and c(e)=c(f) for some edges e,f\in E_{G}(u). 



Proof.



Suppose there exists v\in V(G)-U where E_{G}(v)=\{e,f,g\} and c(e)=c(f). Let G^{\prime}=G/e\backslash f and let G^{\prime\prime}=G^{\prime}/e\backslash f. If e,g belong to a triangle in G having edges e,g,h , then c(g)=c(h) (since (G,c) has no rainbow cycles). Thus we see that either e,g or f,g belong to no triangle of G depending on whether c(e)\neq c(g) or c(f)\neq c(g). Without loss of generality, we may assume that e,g belong to no triangle of G. Then G^{\prime} is simple. Let M^{\prime}=M/e\backslash f. Given that c is stratified, it can be easily shown that G^{\prime}\not\simeq K_{23}^{+} . Thus by assumption there is a T -SRCT \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} for (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) where c^{\prime}=c\big|E(M^{\prime}). If g\not\in C_{1}^{\prime}\cup C_{2}^{\prime}\cup C_{3}^{\prime} , then \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). If one of the circuits contains g , say g\in C_{1}^{\prime} , then for some rainbow circuit C_{1} , E(C_{1})=E(C_{1}^{\prime})+e and \{C_{1},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
∎










(C) We may assume that v_{n-1}=u_{1},\ v_{n-2}=u_{2}, d_{G}(v_{n-1})=d_{G}(v_{n-2})=2. 



Proof.



By (5) and (5),
we have \{v_{n-2},v_{n-2}\}\subset\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\} 
and we may assume that v_{n-1}=u_{1} and v_{n-2}=u_{2}. 
Suppose first that d_{G}(u_{2})=3. Then u_{2}=v_{(n-1)j} for some j , and we may assume that u_{2}=v_{(n-1)1}. Suppose u_{3}\neq v_{(n-1)2}. By Lemma 5, there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{1}^{\prime} and P_{2}^{\prime} in G-u_{1} where for i=1,2, P_{i}^{\prime} has terminal vertices v_{(n-1)i} and u_{3} and |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(G-u_{1})-1=n-2. Let P_{1}=u_{1}u_{2},\ P_{2}=P_{1}^{\prime}, and P_{3}=P_{2}^{\prime}+e_{(n-1)2}+u_{1}. Then P_{i},\ i=1,2,3 are edge-disjoint rainbow paths where for all i<j, |P_{i}|+|P_{j}|\leq n-1. For i=1,2,3, let C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i}. Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be an T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Suppose instead that v_{(n-1)2}=u_{3} (and v_{(n-1)1}=u_{2} ). For i=1,2, let C_{i}=\{f_{i},e_{(n-1)i}\} and let C_{3} be an f_{3} -rainbow circuit for (M-e_{(n-1)1}-e_{(n-1)2},c) containing f_{3} (noting that |C_{3}|\leq r(M-e_{(n-1)1}-e_{(n-1)2})=n-1 ). Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). Thus we may assume that d_{G}(v_{n-2})=d_{G}(u_{2})=2. 
∎










(D) We may assume that u_{3}\not\in\{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cup\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}. 



Proof.



By (7), we may assume that v_{n-1}=u_{1},\ v_{n-2}=u_{2}, d_{G}(v_{n-1})=d_{G}(v_{n-2})=2. 
Suppose that u_{3}\in\{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\} , where we may assume that u_{3}=v_{(n-1)1}. Suppose first that \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}=\mathchar 31. By Lemma 5, there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i},\ i=1,2 where for i=1,2, P_{i} originates at v_{(n-1)i} and terminates at a vertex in \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}, the terminal vertices of P_{i},\ i=1,2 being distinct. Moreover, |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G-u_{1}-u_{2})-2=n-4. Suppose first that for i=1,2 , P_{i} has terminal vertex v_{(n-2)i}. 
Let C_{1}=E(P_{2})+\{f_{1},e_{(n-1)2},e_{(n-2)2}\},\ C_{2}=E(P_{1})+\{f_{2},e_{(n-2)1}\} , and C_{3}=\{f_{3},e_{(n-1)1}\}. Then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq(n-4)+5=n+1 and thus it is seen that \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
If instead for i=1,2, P_{i} has terminal vertex v_{(n-2)(3-i)}, then by a similar construction, one can find a T -SRCT. Suppose instead that \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}\neq\mathchar 31. Suppose first that
 u_{3}\in\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}. We may assume that u_{3}=v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-2)1}. By Lemma 5, there exists a rainbow path P from v_{(n-1)2} to v_{(n-2)2} in G-u_{1}-u_{2} having length at most \lfloor\frac{n-2}{2}\rfloor\leq n-4. 
Let C_{1}=E(P)+\{f_{1},e_{(n-1)2},e_{(n-2)2}\}, C_{2}=\{f_{2},e_{(n-1)1}\}, and C_{3}=\{f_{3},e_{(n-2)1}\}. Then |C_{1}|\leq n-1 and \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). Suppose instead that u_{3}\not\in\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}. 
We may assume that v_{(n-1)2}=v_{(n-2)2}. By Lemma 5, there exists a rainbow path P from u_{3} to v_{(n-2)1} in G-u_{1}-u_{2} having length at most \lfloor\frac{n-2}{2}\rfloor\leq n-4. 
Let C_{1}=\{f_{1},e_{(n-1)2},e_{(n-2)2}\},\ C_{2}=\{f_{2},e_{(n-1)1}\}, and C_{3}=E(P)+\{f_{3},e_{(n-2)1}\}. Then |C_{3}|\leq(n-4)+2=n-2 and hence \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
It follows from the above that we may assume that u_{3}\not\in\{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\} . Given that u_{1} and u_{2} are interchangeable, we may also assume that u_{3}\not\in\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\} .
∎










(E) If \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}=\mathchar 31, then v_{n-3}=u_{3},\ d_{G}(v_{n-3})=2,\ d_{G}(v_{n-4})=4 and we may assume that v_{n-4}=v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-3)1}. 



Proof.



Suppose that \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}=\mathchar 31. Then 
 d_{G}(v_{n-3})\leq 3 and hence v_{n-3}=u_{3}. By (5), we may assume that u_{3}\not\in\{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cup\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}. It follows that d_{G}(u_{3})=2. 
Now d_{G}(v_{n-4})\leq 4. If d_{G}(v_{n-4})\leq 3, then v_{n-4}\in\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}, which is impossible. Thus d_{G}(v_{n-4})=4 and hence v_{n-4} is adjacent to u_{i} and u_{3} for some i\in\{1,2\}. We may assume that v_{n-4} is adjacent to u_{1} and u_{3} and v_{n-4}=v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-3)1}. 
∎










(F) If \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}=\mathchar 31, then we may assume that v_{(n-3)2}=v_{(n-1)2}. 



Proof.



Assume that \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}=\mathchar 31. By (5), we have that v_{n-3}=u_{3},\ d_{G}(u_{3})=2,\ d_{G}(v_{n-4})=4 and we may assume that v_{n-4}=v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-3)1}. 
Suppose that v_{(n-3)2}\neq v_{(n-1)2}. 
Let H=G-u_{1}-u_{3} and let u_{1}^{\prime}=v_{(n-1)2},u_{2}^{\prime}=u_{2}, and u_{3}^{\prime}=v_{(n-3)2}. By Lemma 5, there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{1}^{\prime} and P_{3}^{\prime} in H , where P_{1}^{\prime} joins u_{1}^{\prime} to u_{2}^{\prime} , P_{3}^{\prime} joins u_{2}^{\prime} to u_{3}^{\prime} and |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{3}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(H)-1=n-3. Let P_{1} be the rainbow path in G where E(P_{1})=E(P_{1}^{\prime})+e_{(n-1)2}+u_{1} and let P_{3} be the rainbow path in G where E(P_{3})=E(P_{3}^{\prime})+e_{(n-3)2}+u_{3}. Let P_{2}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)1}v_{(n-1)1}e_{(n-3)1}u_{3}. 
We have |P_{1}|+|P_{3}|\leq n-1 and hence |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq n-1 and |P_{2}|+|P_{3}|\leq n-1. For i=1,2,3, let C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i} . Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Thus we can assume that v_{(n-3)2}=v_{(n-1)2}. 
∎










(G) We may assume that \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}\neq\mathchar 31. 



Proof.



Suppose \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}=\mathchar 31. By (5) and (7), we may assume that v_{n-4}=v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-3)1} and v_{(n-3)2}=v_{(n-1)2}. Let H=G-U-v_{n-4} .
By Lemma 5, there are edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{1}^{\prime} and P_{3}^{\prime} in H , where P_{1}^{\prime} joins v_{(n-1)2} to v_{(n-2)1} , P_{3}^{\prime} joins v_{(n-1)2} to v_{(n-2)2} and |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{3}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(H)-1=n-5. Let P_{1} be the rainbow path in G where E(P_{1})=E(P_{1}^{\prime})+e_{(n-1)2}+e_{(n-2)1} and let P_{3} be the rainbow path in G where E(P_{3})=E(P_{3}^{\prime})+e_{(n-2)2}+e_{(n-3)1}. Let P_{2}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)1}v_{(n-1)1}e_{(n-3)1}u_{3}. Letting C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i} , we see that \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
∎










(H) We may assume that for j=1,2, v_{(n-1)j}=v_{(n-2)j}. 



Proof.



By (5), we may assume that \{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}\}\cap\{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}\neq\mathchar 31. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
 v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-2)1}. 
Suppose that v_{(n-1)2}\neq v_{(n-2)2}. Assume first that u_{3}\not\in N_{G}(\{u_{1},u_{2}\}). For j=1,2, let u_{j}^{\prime}=v_{(n-j)2}, and let u_{3}^{\prime}=u_{3}. By Lemma 5, there edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{2}^{\prime} and P_{3}^{\prime} in G-u_{1}-u_{2} , joining u_{3}^{\prime} to u_{1}^{\prime} and u_{2}^{\prime} , respectively, where |P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{2}^{\prime}|\leq n-3. Let P_{1}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)1}v_{(n-1)1}e_{(n-2)1}u_{2} , and P_{2} and P_{3} be the rainbow paths where P_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}+e_{(n-1)2+u_{1}} and P_{3}=P_{3}^{\prime}+e_{(n-2)2}+u_{2}. 
Then P_{i},\ i=1,2,3 are edge-disjoint rainbow paths where for all i<j, |P_{i}|+|P_{j}|\leq n-1. For i=1,2,3, let C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i} . Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Assume now that u_{3}\in N_{G}(\{u_{1},u_{2}\}). Note that here n\geq 6. Suppose first that u_{3}=v_{(n-1)1}. Then v_{n-3}=u_{3}; otherwise, d_{G}(v_{n-3})\leq 3 implying that v_{n-3}\in U , a contradiction.
By Lemma 5, there is a rainbow path P in G-U from v_{(n-1)2} to v_{(n-2)2} of length at most \lfloor\frac{n-3}{2}\rfloor\leq n-4 (since n\geq 6 ).
Let P_{1} be the path where E(P_{1})=E(P)+\{e_{(n-1)2},e_{(n-2)2}\} . Let P_{2}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)1}u_{3} and P_{3}=u_{2}e_{(n-2)1}u_{3}. For i=1,2,3 let C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i}. Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Suppose instead that u_{3}\in\{v_{(n-1)2},v_{(n-2)2}\}. We may assume that u_{3}=v_{(n-1)2}. By Lemma 5, there is a rainbow path P in G-u_{1}-u_{2} from u_{3} to v_{(n-2)2} of length at most \lfloor\frac{n-2}{2}\rfloor\leq n-4 (since n\geq 6 ).
Let P_{1}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)1}v_{(n-1)1}e_{(n-2)1}u_{2} , P_{2}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)2}u_{3} , and let P_{3} be path where P_{3}=P+e_{(n-2)2}+u_{2}. For i=1,2,3 let C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i} . Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Thus by the above, we may assume that v_{(n-1)2}=v_{(n-2)2}. 
∎










(I) We may assume that v_{n-3}=u_{3}. 



Proof.



By (5), we may assume that for j=1,2, v_{(n-1)j}=v_{(n-2)j}. If u_{3}\in N_{G}(u_{1}), then finding a F -SRCT is straightforward. Thus we may assume that u_{3}\not\in N_{G}(u_{1}). 




Suppose that v_{n-3}\neq u_{3}. Then d_{G}(v_{n-3})\geq 4, for otherwise, (5) and (5) would imply that v_{n-3}\in U, a contradiction. It now follows that v_{n-3}\in\{v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2}. 
By symmetry, we may assume that v_{n-3}=v_{(n-1)2}. If \{v_{11},v_{12}\}\neq\{v_{(n-1)1},u_{3}\}, then by Lemma 5, there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{2}^{\prime} and P_{3}^{\prime} in G-u_{1}-u_{2}-v_{n-3} between u_{3} and v_{(n-1)1} where |P_{2}^{\prime}|+|P_{3}^{\prime}|\leq n-3. Now let P_{1}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)2}v_{(n-1)2}e_{(n-2)2}u_{2} , P_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}+e_{(n-1)1}+u_{1} and P_{3}=P_{3}^{\prime}+e_{(n-1)2}+u_{2}. Then P_{i},\ i=1,2,3 are edge-disjoint rainbow paths where for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, |P_{i}|+|P_{j}|\leq n-1. We now see that
for C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i},\ i=1,2,3, \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCT for (M,c). Suppose on the other hand that \{v_{11},v_{12}\}=\{v_{(n-1)1},u_{3}\}. Then we may assume that v_{11}=v_{(n-1)1} and v_{12}=u_{3}. 
If n\geq 6, then we see that d_{G}(v_{n-4})\leq 3 and hence v_{n-4}\in U. However, this would imply that v_{n-4}=u_{3} , which is not possible since u_{3}=v_{12}. Thus n=5 and we see that G\simeq K_{23}^{+}, contradicting our assumptions. Thus we may assume that v_{n-3}=u_{3}. 
∎





By (5), we may assume that u_{3}=v_{n-3}. Again, if u_{3}\in N_{G}(u_{1}), then it is straightforward to find a F -SRCT. Thus we may assume that u_{3}\not\in N_{G}(u_{1}). Since u_{3}=v_{n-3}, it follows that d_{G}(u_{3})=2. It is now seen that the roles of u_{1},u_{2}, and u_{3} are interchangeable and one could for example assume that v_{n-2}=u_{3} and v_{n-3}=u_{2}. Because of this, we may assume that N_{G}(u_{3})=N_{G}(u_{1}) and for j=1,2, v_{(n-3)j}=v_{(n-1)j}. 
If n=5, then G\simeq K_{23}^{+}, contradicting our assumptions. Thus n\geq 6. By Lemma 5, there is a rainbow path P between v_{(n-1)1} and v_{(n-1)2} in G-U of length at most \lfloor\frac{n-3}{2}\rfloor\leq n-5 (since n\geq 6 ). Let P_{1} be the path where E(P_{1})=E(P)+e_{(n-1)2}+e_{(n-2)1} and let
 P_{2}=u_{1}e_{(n-1)1}v_{(n-1)1}e_{(n-3)1}u_{3} and P_{3}=u_{2}e_{(n-2)2}v_{(n-2)2}e_{(n-3)2}u_{3}. Then P_{i},\ i=1,2,3 are edge-disjoint rainbow paths where for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, |P_{i}|+|P_{j}|\leq n-1. For i=1,2,3, let C_{i}=E(P_{i})+f_{i}. Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). This completes the proof.
∎





5.1 Proof of Theorem 2 for M=M(G) and \varepsilon(M)=2r(M)-1 




Let T=\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\} and H=G+T where in H , f_{1}=u_{1}u_{2},\ f_{2}=u_{1}u_{3}, and f_{3}=u_{2}u_{3}. Let M=M(G). 
Suppose first that G\not\simeq K_{23}^{+}. Then Lemma 5 implies that (M,c) has a T -SRCT \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} where f_{i}\in E(C_{i}),\ i=1,2,3. 
On the other hand, suppose G\simeq K_{23}^{+}. Then V(G)=\{v_{11},v_{12},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}. For at least one j\in[2],\ d_{G}(v_{1j})=4 and we may assume that d_{G}(v_{11})=4. Then there are two possible graphs for G (up to re-colouring) as illustrated in Figure 1.

 v_{11} v_{12} v_{2} v_{3} v_{4} 1 2 3 4 2 3 4 v_{11} v_{2} v_{12} v_{3} v_{4} 2 1 3 4 2 3 4 
Figure 1: The two possible graphs for G\simeq K_{23}^{+}. 




Suppose G is the first graph in Figure 1. If \{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}\neq\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}, then it is readily seen that one can find a T -SRCT. On the other hand, if \{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}=\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\} , then it is seen that for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, there is a T -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} where f_{i}\in C_{1}, f_{j}\in C_{2} and e_{1}\not\in C_{1}\cup C_{2}. A similar argument can be used for the second graph in Figure 1 and
it follows that
Theorem 2 i) holds for M=M(G). 




To prove Theorem 2 ii) is true for M=M(G), suppose N=M+x is a graphic extension of M where x and e=e_{1} are non-parallel. Let x=uv be the corresponding edge added to G. By Lemma 5, there exist two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{i},\ i=1,2 in G from u to v where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq n. For i=1,2 let C_{i}=E(P_{i})+x. Then C_{i},\ i=1,2 are circuits in N where E(C_{1})\cap E(C_{2})=\{x\} and |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n+2=r(M)+3. It follows that C_{i},\ i=1,2 is an x -semi-SRCP for (M,c). This proves part ii) for (M,c). 






6 Circuit-achromatic cographic matroids M with 2r(M)-1 elements




In this section, we prove Theorem 2 for cographic matroids M where \varepsilon(M)=2r(M)-1. For the remainder this section, we assume that M=M^{*}(G) is a connected simple cographic matroid where r(M)=n-1 and \varepsilon(M)=2(n-1)-1=2n-3. We may also assume that M is connected, meaning that G is also connected.
Let c:E(G)\rightarrow[n-1] be an (n-1) - colouring of M such that |c^{-1}(1)|=1 and for i=2,\dots,n-1 , |c^{-1}(i)|=2. Assume that (M,c) is circuit-achromatic; that is, G has no rainbow cocycles.
By Theorem 3, the colouring is stratified. For i=1,\dots,n-1 , let X_{i}=c^{-1}(i) where X_{1}=\{e_{1}\} and for i\geq 2 , X_{i}=\{e_{i1},e_{i2}\}. 




6.1 Stratified Colourings in Co-Graphic Matroids




In this section, we discuss how one constructs a stratified colouring in a cographic matroid. First, we will describe how one constructs a cographic extension of cographic matroid M by a single element. We do this by introducing a splitting operation which is the reverse of contraction. Let H be a graph and let v\in V(H). Partition E_{H}(v) into two non-empty sets E_{1} and E_{2}. Let H^{\prime} be the graph obtained from H as follows: the graph H^{\prime} has vertex set V(H^{\prime})=V(H)-v+\{v_{1},v_{2}\} and edge set E(H^{\prime})=E(H)+f_{1}+\cdots+f_{k}. Here if e\in E(H)-E_{H}(v) , then e has the same endvertices in H^{\prime} as in H. For i=1,2, if e=uv\in E_{i}, then e has endvertices u and v_{i} in H^{\prime}. 
In H^{\prime} , the edges f_{i},\ i\in[k] are parallel and have endvertices v_{1} and v_{2}. We say that H^{\prime} is obtained from H by splitting v and adding f_{1},\dots,f_{k} and we write H\xrightarrow[v]{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}H^{\prime} . When v is not specified, we will simply write
 H\xrightarrow{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}H^{\prime}. See Figure 2.

v E_{1} E_{2} v_{1} v_{2} \scriptstyle{f_{1}} \scriptstyle{f_{k}} 
Figure 2: The operation H\xrightarrow[v]{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}H^{\prime} 





6.2 The construction of G 




Let \nu(G)=m . We have r^{*}(M)=r(M(G))=m-1, and thus m-1=\varepsilon(M)-(n-1)=n-2 and m=n-1. Since c is stratified, we may assume that G is constructed as follows:
Let G_{1} be the graph consisting of the single loop e_{1} incident with a vertex v_{1}. Next, let G_{2} be the graph obtained from G_{1} by changing e_{1} to an edge e_{1}=v_{11}v_{12} and then adding two parallel edges e_{21}=v_{11}v_{12} and e_{22}=v_{11}v_{12}. Continuing, let G_{3} be obtained from G_{2} by splitting one of the vertices v_{11} or v_{12} and adding edges e_{31} and e_{32} ; that is G_{2}\xrightarrow{e_{31},e_{32}}G_{3}. Continuing, we define G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{n-1} where
 G_{i-1}\xrightarrow{e_{i1},e_{i2}}G_{i}. 
Then G=G_{n-1} . It is easy to see that for i=1,\dots,n-1, M(G_{i}^{*}) has no rainbow circuits (i.e. G_{i} has no rainbow cocycles) and r(M(G_{i}^{*}))=i. We will make frequent use of the next observation whose easy inductive proof we leave to the reader.









6.1 Observation




For all i\geq 2 , G_{i} has no loops, the maximum vertex degree in G_{i} is at most i+1 and for all pairs of distinct vertices u,v\in V(G_{i}),\ d_{G_{i}}(u)+d_{G_{i}}(v)\leq i+4. 




We shall first establish Theorem 2 ii) for M . Let H be the graph obtained from G by splitting a vertex v and adding an edge x ; that is, G\xrightarrow[v]{x}H and N=M^{*}(H) is a cographic extension of M by an element x. 
Given that \nu(H)=\nu(G)+1=m+1=n and \varepsilon(H)=\varepsilon(G)+1, we see that r(N)=r(M)=n-1 .









6.2 Lemma




If r(M)\geq 2 and x and e=e_{1} are non-parallel, then there is an x -semi-SRCP for (M,c). 



Proof.



By induction on r(M). The lemma is seen to be true when r(M)=2. We may assume the lemma is true for such cographic matroids of rank less than n-1. 
Suppose that for some i , the edges e_{i1} and e_{i2} form a monochromatic digon (i.e. 2 -cycle) in G. Let M^{\prime}=M/e_{i1}\backslash e_{i2} and N^{\prime}=N/e_{i1}\backslash e_{i2}. Let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(M^{\prime}). 
We observe that any circuit of N^{\prime} is also a circuit of N . As such, x and e_{1} are non-parallel in N^{\prime}. By assumption, there exists an x -semi-SRCP for (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) and such is also seen to be a x -semi-SRCP for (M,c). Thus we may assume that G contains no monochromatic digons.
Given that |c(M)|=n-1 and e_{1} is a colour-singular edge, there are exactly n-2=\nu(H)-2 colour classes with two elements. Since by assumption no two edges in any such colour class form a monochromatic digon in G, it follows that H-x has
at least two rainbow vertices, say x_{1} and x_{2}. Given that G=H/x contains no rainbow vertices (because (M,c) is circuit-achromatic), it follows that in H , x=x_{1}x_{2}. Now by Observation 6.2, d_{G}(v)=d_{G_{n-1}}(v)\leq n , it follows that d_{H}(x_{1})+d_{H}(x_{2})\leq n+2=r(M)+3. For i=1,2, let C_{i}\subseteq E_{H}(x_{i}) be a circuit of N containing x. Then \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is seen to be an x -semi-SRCP for (M,c). ∎






6.3 Completing the proof of Theorem 2 i) for M 




To complete the proof of Theorem 2 i) for M , it will suffice to prove the following:









6.3 Lemma




Suppose G\xrightarrow[v^{1}]{e^{1}}G^{1}\xrightarrow[v^{2}]{e^{2}}G^{2}\xrightarrow[v^{3}]{e^{3}}G^{3} where T=\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3}\} is a co-independent circuit of N=M^{*}(G^{3}). 
Extend the colouring c to G^{3} where c(e^{1})=n,\ c(e^{2})=n+1 and c(e^{3})=n+2. 
Then there are three edge-disjoint rainbow cocycles C_{i},\ i\in[3] in G^{3} where e^{i}\in C_{i},\ i=1,2,3 and for all i<j , |C_{i}|+|C_{j}|\leq r(M)+2=n+1. That is, \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T-SRCT for (M,c). 



Proof.



In G^{3} let e^{i}=u_{1i}u_{2i},\ i\in[3]. For i=1,2 we may assume that u_{i1},u_{i2},u_{i3} belong to the same component of G^{3}-T. For i=1,2, let U_{i} be the set of vertices in the component containing u_{ij},\ j\in[3]. For all elements x\in E(N) which are not colour-singular, we let x^{\prime} denote the other element in the colour class containing x. 









(A) There is no element x\in E(G) and subset T^{\prime}\subseteq T for which x\cup T^{\prime} is the edge set of a cycle in G^{3}. 



Proof.



Suppose there is a cycle C in G^{3} for which E(C)=T^{\prime}+x for some subset T^{\prime}\subseteq T and element x\in E(G). Then C/T^{\prime} is loop x in G , a contradiction since G has no loops by Observation 6.2.
∎










(B) We may assume that (G^{3},c) has no monochromatic digons.



Proof.



Suppose (G^{3},c) has a monochromatic digon with edges e,f. Let H=G/e\backslash f and H^{3}=G^{3}/e\backslash f. We see that H\xrightarrow{e^{1}}H^{1}\xrightarrow{e^{2}}H^{2}\xrightarrow{e^{3}}H^{3}. 
Since T is a cocyle of G^{3}, it is a cocycle of H^{3}. Then H and H^{3} satisfy the conditions of the lemma. Thus the lemma holds for H^{3} and hence it contains 3 edge-disjoint rainbow cocycles C_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2,3 where e^{i}\in C_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2,3 and for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3 , |C_{i}|+|C_{j}|\leq(r(M)-1)+2=r(M)+1. Since C_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2,3 are also cocycles of G^{3}, the lemma holds for G^{3}. Thus we may assume that (G^{3},c) has no monochromatic digons.
∎





By (6.3), we may assume that G^{3} has no monochromatic digons.
It follows that for each colour class X_{i} , there is at most one vertex v\in V(G^{3}) for which X_{i}\subseteq E_{G^{3}}(v). 
Given that there are only r(M)-1=n-2=m-1=\nu(G^{3})-4 colour classes with 2 elements, there are at least 4 rainbow vertices in G^{3} . Furthermore, since G contains no rainbow cocycles, each rainbow vertex of G^{3} is one the vertices u_{ij}\ i\in[3],\ j\in[2]. 









(C) We may assume that for i=1,2, |U_{i}|\geq 2. 



Proof.



Suppose that for some i, |U_{i}|=1. We may assume that U_{1}=\{u\}. Then u=u_{11}=u_{12}=u_{13} and u,u_{21},u_{22},u_{23} are rainbow vertices in G^{3}. Since G has no loops, none of the vertices u_{21},u_{22},u_{23} are adjacent. We also have that for j=1,2,3, d_{G^{3}}(u_{2j})\geq 2 . Furthermore, since (by Observation 6.2) the degree of any vertex in G is at most r(M)+1=n , it follows that for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, d_{G^{3}}(u_{2i})+d_{G^{3}}(u_{2j})\leq n+1=r(M)+2. Letting C_{i}=E_{G^{3}}(u_{2i}),\ i=1,2,3 , it is seen that \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCyP for (M,c). Thus we may assume that for i=1,2, |U_{i}|\geq 2. 
∎










(D) If for some i\in\{1,2\} and distinct j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,2,3\} , u_{ij_{1}}=u_{ij_{2}} , then we may assume that the vertices u_{(3-i)j},\ j\in[3], are non-distinct.



Proof.



Suppose that for some i\in\{1,2\} and distinct j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,2,3\} , u_{ij_{1}}=u_{ij_{2}} and the vertices u_{(3-i)j},\ j\in[3], are distinct.
We may assume that
 u=u_{11}=u_{12} and the vertices u_{2j},\ j\in[3] are distinct. By (6.3), we may assume that u_{13}\neq u. We see that v^{3}\in V(G) and hence by Observation 6.2, d_{G}(v^{1})+d_{G}(v^{3})\leq n+3. 
Since \{e^{1},e^{2}\} is not a cocycle of G^{3} , we have that d_{G^{3}}(u)\geq 3. Furthermore, (6.3) implies that u_{21} and u_{22} are non-adjacent in G^{3}. At most one of the vertices u,u_{13} and u_{2j},\ j\in[3] is not a rainbow vertex. Suppose that u_{2j},\ j=1,2 are rainbow vertices.




Suppose that u is not a rainbow vertex (and u_{13} and u_{2j},\ j\in[2] are rainbow vertices).
We have




 \displaystyle d_{G}(v^{1}) 
 \displaystyle=d_{G^{3}}(u)+d_{G^{3}}(u_{21})+d_{G^{3}}(u_{22})-4 



 \displaystyle d_{G^{3}}(u_{21})+d_{G^{3}}(u_{22}) 
 \displaystyle=d_{G}(v^{1})-d_{G^{3}}(u)+4 




 \displaystyle\leq d_{G}(v^{1})+1\leq n+1=r(M)+2. 



Suppose that d_{G^{3}}(u_{13})=2. Then u_{13} is a rainbow vertex where for j=1,2, d_{G^{3}}(u_{2j})+d_{G^{3}}(u_{13})\leq r(M)+2. Letting C_{j}=E_{G^{3}}(u_{2j}),\ j\in[2], and C_{3}=E_{G^{3}}(u_{13}), one sees that \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCyT for (M,c). Suppose that d_{G^{3}}(u_{13})\geq 3. If d_{G^{3}}(u_{23})\geq 3, then d_{G^{3}}(u_{13})\leq d_{G}(v^{3})-1 and hence for j=1,2 ,




 d_{G^{3}}(u_{2j})+d_{G^{3}}(u_{13})\leq d_{G}(v^{1})-1+d_{G}(v^{3})-1\leq r(M)+2. 



We can now define C_{i},\ i\in[3] as before.




Suppose u is a rainbow vertex in G^{3}. Define C_{i},\ i=1,2 as before and define C_{3}=E_{G^{3}}(U_{1}-u). Then C_{i},\ i\in[3] are pairwise disjoint rainbow cocycles and for j=1,2, |C_{j}|+|C_{3}|\leq d_{G}(v^{1})+1\leq r(M)+2. 
Thus \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyT for (M,c). 




From the above, we may assume that for some j\in[2], u_{2j} is not a rainbow vertex. Then u and u_{i3},\ i=1,2 are rainbow vertices. Since G has no rainbow vertices, v^{3} is not a rainbow vertex and E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{13},u_{23}\}) contains a colour class \{y,y^{\prime}\}. Then y and y^{\prime} are non-incident and hence G^{3} must have at least 5 rainbow vertices, implying that u is a rainbow vertex. We can now argue as before.
∎










(E) We may assume that the vertices u_{ij},\ i\in[2],\ j\in[3] are distinct.



Proof.



Suppose the vertices u_{ij},\ i\in[2],\ j\in[3] are not distinct. We may assume that
 u=u_{11}=u_{12}. By (6.3), we may assume that u_{13}\neq u . By (6.3), we may assume that for some 1\leq i<j\leq 3, u_{2i}=u_{2j}. Noting that u_{21}\neq u_{22}, we may assume that v=u_{22}=u_{23}. Given that G^{3} has at least 4 rainbow vertices, it follows that all the vertices u,v,u_{21},u_{13} are rainbow vertices and both pairs of vertices u,u_{13} and u_{21},v are nonadjacent in G^{3} (by (6.3)).
Let C_{1}=E_{G^{3}}(U_{2}-v),\ C_{2}=E_{G^{3}}(U_{1}-u_{13}+u_{21}),\ C_{3}=E_{G^{3}}(U_{1}-u). Given d_{G}(v^{1})\leq r(M)+1 , it follows that for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3 , |C_{i}|+|C_{j}|\leq r(M)+2 and \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyT for (M,c). 
∎





By (6.3), we may assume that all the vertices u_{ij},\ i\in[2],\ j\in[3] are distinct. Then v^{j},\ j=1,2,3 are distinct vertices of G. For all i,j, let D_{ij}=E_{G^{3}}(u_{ij}). Since there are at least 4 rainbow vertices, we may assume that u_{11} and u_{21} are rainbow vertices.









(F) We may assume that the vertices u_{1j},\ j\in[3] and u_{2j},\ j\in[2] are rainbow vertices in G^{3}. 



Proof.



Since G has no rainbow vertices, it follows that E_{G}(v^{1})=D_{11}\triangle D_{21}=E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{11},u_{21}\}) contains a colour class, say \{x,x^{\prime}\} , where we may assume that x\in D_{11} and x\in D_{21}. Then x and x^{\prime} are non-incident and
as such, there must be at least 5 rainbow
vertices in G^{3} . Thus we may assume that u_{1j},\ j=1,2,3 and u_{2j},\ j=1,2 are rainbow vertices.
∎










(G) We may assume that for some i\in[2], at least one pair of the vertices in \{u_{ij}\ \big|\ j\in[3]\} is adjacent.



Proof.



Suppose that for i=1,2, the vertices in \{u_{ij}\ \big|\ j\in[3]\} are pairwise non-adjacent.
For i=1,2,3 let




 C_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}D_{1i}&\mathrm{if}\ d_{G^{3}}(u_{2i})>2\\
D_{2i}&\mathrm{if}\ d_{G^{3}}(u_{2i})=2\end{array}\right. 



Given that each vertex of G has degree at most r(M)+1 , it follows that for j=1,2,3 , either d_{G^{3}}(u_{1j})\leq r(M) or d_{G^{3}}(u_{2j})=2. Since it follows by Observation 6.2 that for any two vertices x,y in V(G) , d_{G}(x)+d_{G}(y)\leq r(M)+4, one can check that for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, |C_{i}|+|C_{j}|\leq r(M)+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCyT for (M,c). 
∎










(H) We may assume that all the vertices u_{ij},\ i\in[2],\ j\in[3] are rainbow vertices.



Proof.



By (6.3), we may assume that u_{1j},\ j\in[3] and u_{2j},\ j\in[2] are rainbow vertices in G^{3}. Assume that G^{3} only has 5 rainbow vertices and u_{23} is not a rainbow vertex.
Then d_{G^{3}}(u_{23})\geq 3 . Recall that \{x,x^{\prime}\} is a colour class where x\in D_{11} and x^{\prime}\in D_{21}. Using the same argument for D_{12}\triangle D_{22} as we did for D_{11}\triangle D_{21}, it follows that there is a colour class, say \{y,y^{\prime}\} ,where
 y\in D_{12} and y^{\prime}\in D_{22}. If \{x,x^{\prime}\}\neq\{y,y^{\prime}\} , then G^{3} must have at least 6 rainbow vertices, a contradiction. Thus y=x and y^{\prime}=x^{\prime} and hence x=u_{11}u_{12} and x^{\prime}=u_{21}u_{22}. 
Since T does not properly contain a cocycle and M is simple (and hence has no 2 -cocycles), there exists (i,j)\in[2]\times[2] such that d_{G^{3}}(u_{ij})\geq 3 and d_{G^{3}}(u_{(3-i)(3-j)})\geq 3. We may assume that this is true for i=j=1. Suppose that u_{13} is not adjacent to u_{12}. 
Let C_{1}=D_{21},\ C_{2}=D_{12} and C_{3}=D_{13}. Then for all i\in[3],\ |C_{i}|\leq d_{G}(v^{i})-1 and for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, |C_{i}|+|C_{j}|\leq d_{G}(v^{i})+d_{G}(v^{j})-2\leq r(M)+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCyT for (M,c). 
Suppose that u_{13} is adjacent to u_{12} and let z=u_{12}u_{13}. If z\neq e_{1}, then z and z^{\prime} are non-incident edges and it follows that G^{3} contains at least 6 rainbow vertices, a contradiction. Thus z=e_{1}. This also shows that u_{13} is not adjacent to u_{11}. 
If d_{G^{3}}(u_{21})\geq 3 , then defining C_{1}=D_{11},\ C_{2}=D_{21} and C_{3}=D_{13}, we see that as before \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCyT for (M,c). Thus we may assume that d_{G^{3}}(u_{21})=2. Now define C_{1}=D_{21},\ C_{2}=D_{12} and
 C_{3}=E_{G^{3}}(U_{2}-u_{21}-u_{22}). Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyT for (M,c). 
∎





By (6.3), G^{3} has (exactly) 6 rainbow vertices which are the vertices u_{ij},\ i\in[2],\ j\in[3]. Since M=M^{*}(G) is simple, G has no 2 -cocycles and thus for all j\in[3],\ d_{G^{3}}(u_{1j})+d_{G^{3}}(u_{2j})\geq 5. Moreover, since (M,c) is circuit-achromatic, for all j\in[3], there is a colour class \{f_{1j},f_{2j}\} where \{f_{1j},f_{2j}\}\subseteq E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{1j},u_{2j}\}) and for i\in[2] and j\in[3], f_{ij}\in D_{ij}. 




For a function \phi:[3]\rightarrow[2] we define S_{\phi}=\{u_{\phi(i)i}\ \big|\ i=1,2,3\}. The set S_{\phi} is said to be short if it is independent and for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, |D_{\phi(i)i}|+|D_{\phi(j)j}|\leq r(M)+2. For the function \phi , we define its counterpart \phi^{\prime}:[3]\rightarrow[2] where for all i, \phi^{\prime}(i)=3-\phi(i). We let S_{\phi}^{\prime}=S_{\phi_{i}^{\prime}}. 
The next observation, whose proof we omit, follows from the fact that r(M)\geq 3 and for all x,y\in V(G), d_{G}(x)+d_{G}(y)\leq r(M)+4. 









(I) Let \phi:[3]\rightarrow[2] where S_{\phi} is independent. For all 1\leq i<j\leq 3 , if
