 |D_{\phi(i)i}|+|D_{\phi(j)j}|\leq 5\ \mathrm{or}\ |D_{\phi^{\prime}(i)i}|+|D_{\phi^{\prime}(j)j}|\geq 6\ \ \ (\star), 



then |D_{\phi(i)i}|+|D_{\phi(j)j}|\leq r(M)+2. Thus if ( \star ) holds for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, then S_{\phi} is short.




Since G^{3} has 6 rainbow vertices, only edges belonging to at most 3 colour classes in (G,c) can join pairs of vertices among u_{ij}\ i\in[2],\ j\in[3] and in the case that there are 3 such colour classes, one of them is X_{1}=\{e_{1}\} . Thus at least two of the colour classes \{f_{1j},f_{2j}\},\ j\in[3] are equal. We may assume that f_{11}=f_{12} and f_{21}=f_{22}. 
Also, for some i\in\{1,2\}, at least two vertices of u_{ij},\ j\in[3] are non-adjacent. We may assume that u_{11} and u_{13} are non-adjacent. Let \phi_{1}:[3]\rightarrow[2] where for i\in\{1,3\},\ \phi_{1}(i)=1 and \phi_{1}(2)=2. Let S_{1}=S_{\phi_{1}}=\{u_{11},u_{22},u_{13}\} and let S_{1}^{\prime}=S_{\phi_{1}}^{\prime}=\{u_{21},u_{12},u_{23}\}. Let \phi_{2}:[3]\rightarrow[2] where \phi_{2}(1)=2 and for i\in\{2,3\}, \phi_{2}(i)=1. Let S_{2}=S_{\phi_{2}}=\{u_{21},u_{12},u_{13}\} and S_{2}^{\prime}=S_{\phi_{2}}^{\prime}. Then S_{1} is independent. We may assume that for i=1,2, S_{i} and S_{i}^{\prime} are not short. Given that S_{1} is not short, it follows by (6.3) that at least one of the cocyles D_{21},D_{12}, or D_{23} is a 2 -cocycle.









(J) We may assume that |D_{21}|\geq 3. 



Proof.



Suppose |D_{21}|=2. Then |D_{22}|\geq 3 ; otherwise, E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{21},u_{22}\})=\{e^{1},e^{2}\}\subset T is a cocycle of G^{3}. Since |D_{22}|\geq 3, it follows that |D_{12}|\leq d_{G}(v^{2})-1. We also have that |D_{11}|\geq 3; otherwise, E_{G}(v^{1})=\{f_{11},f_{21}\} which is impossible since G has no 2 -cocycles. Then S_{1}^{\prime} is seen to be an independent set.
It follows by (6.3) that |D_{13}|=2. If |D_{23}|=2, then we have |D_{13}|=|D_{23}|=2 and hence d_{g}(v^{3})=2 , contradicting the assumption that G has no 2 -cocycles.
Thus |D_{23}|\geq 3. 
If u_{12} and u_{13} are non-adjacent, then S_{2} is independent and it is seen to be short by (6.3). Thus we may assume that f_{13}=u_{12}u_{13}. 




Suppose f_{23} is not incident with u_{22}. Since E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{11},u_{12},u_{21},u_{22}\}) is seen to be a cocycle of G , it must contain a colour class, say \{f,f^{\prime}\} of (G,c). Now f and f^{\prime} must be incident and we may assume that f\in D_{11} and f^{\prime}\in D_{12}. 
Then |D_{12}|\geq 4. Since |D_{22}|\geq 3, it follows that d_{G}(v^{2})\geq 5. Since d_{G}(v^{1})+d_{G}(v^{2})\leq r(M)+4, it follows that |D_{11}|=d_{G}(v^{1})\leq r(M)-1. Thus |D_{11}|+|D_{13}|\leq r(M)+1. By (6.3), we have that for all j\in\{1,3\} , |D_{1j}|+|D_{22}|\leq r(M)+2 , implying that S_{1} is short - a contradiction.
It follows that f_{23}=u_{22}u_{23}. 




If for some i\in\{1,2\}, |D_{i2}|\geq 4, then d_{G}(v^{2})\geq 5 and one can show that either S_{1} or S_{1}^{\prime} is short using similar arguments as before. Thus for all i\in[2], |D_{i2}|=3. Given that E_{G}(\{v^{1},v^{2},v^{3}\}) is a cocycle in G , it contains a colour class \{f,f^{\prime}\} where f\in E_{G}(v^{1}) and f^{\prime}\in E_{G}(v^{3}). In G^{3}, we have f\in E_{G^{3}}(u_{11}) and f^{\prime}\in E_{G^{3}}(u_{23}) and as such, f and f^{\prime} are non-incident. This yields a contradiction since there are at most two such colour classes.
It follows from the above that we may assume that |D_{21}|\geq 3. 
∎










(K) We may assume that |D_{12}|\geq 3. 



Proof.



By (6.3), we may assume that |D_{21}|\geq 3. Suppose |D_{12}|=2 . Then |D_{11}|\geq 3 and |D_{22}|\geq 3. 
Furthermore, u_{12} and u_{13} are non-adjacent and S_{2}=\{u_{21},u_{12},u_{13}\} is independent. If |D_{23}|\geq 3, then it follows by (6.3) that S_{2} is short. Thus we may assume that |D_{23}|=2. 
Suppose u_{21} and u_{23} are non-adjacent. Then S_{1}^{\prime} is independent. Since |D_{11}|+|D_{21}|-2=d_{G}(v^{1})\leq r(M)+1, it follows that |D_{21}|\leq r(M). Since |D_{12}|=|D_{23}|=2, it follows that S_{1}^{\prime} is short, a contradiction. Thus we may assume that u_{21} and u_{23} are adjacent and f_{23}=u_{21}u_{23}. If |D_{13}|=2, then the previous argument shows that S_{2} is short . Thus we may assume that |D_{13}|\geq 3. 
We see that E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{11},u_{12},u_{21},u_{22}\}) is a cocycle of G and hence it contains a colour class \{f,f^{\prime}\} of (G,c) where f and f^{\prime} are incident. We may assume that f\in D_{21} and f^{\prime}\in D_{22}. 
It follows that |D_{21}|\geq 4 and d_{G}(v^{1})\geq 5. This in turn implies that d_{G}(v^{2})\leq r(M)+4-d_{G}(v^{1})\leq r(M)-1 and hence |D_{22}|\leq r(M)-1. Now it follows by (6.3) that
 S_{2}^{\prime}=\{u_{11},u_{22},u_{23}\} is short, yielding a contradiction. It follows that we may assume that |D_{12}|\geq 3. 
∎





By (6.3) and (6.3), we may assume that |D_{21}|\geq 3 and |D_{12}|\geq 3. We may also assume that |D_{23}|=2, for otherwise it follows by (6.3) that S_{1} is short. We now have that |D_{13}|\geq 3. If
 u_{22} and u_{23} are non-adjacent, then S_{2}^{\prime} is independent and it follows by (6.3) that it is short.
Thus we may assume that f_{23}=u_{22}u_{23}. Now u_{21} and u_{23} are non-adjacent and S_{1}^{\prime} is independent. If |D_{11}|\geq 3, then it follows by (6.3) that S_{1}^{\prime} is short. Thus we may assume that |D_{11}|=2. 
We have d_{G}(v^{2})=|D_{12}|+|D_{22}|-2\geq 4 and as such |D_{21}|=d_{G}(v^{1})\leq r(M)+4-d_{G}(v^{2})\leq r(M). 




If |D_{22}|\geq 4, then it follows by (6.3) that S_{1}^{\prime}=\{u_{21},u_{12},u_{23}\} . Thus we may assume that |D_{22}|=3. 
If f_{13}\neq u_{12}u_{13}, then the cocycle E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{11},u_{12},u_{21},u_{22}\}) contains a colour class \{f,f^{\prime}\} of (G,c) where f and f^{\prime} are incident and we may assume that f\in D_{21} and f^{\prime}\in D_{22}. 
Then |D_{22}|\geq 4, contradicting our assumptions. Thus f_{13}=u_{12}u_{13}. Given that we may assume that |D_{22}|=3, by symmetry we may also assume that |D_{12}|=3. Now E_{G^{3}}(\{u_{ij}\ \big|\ i\in[2],\ j\in[3]\}) is a cocycle of G and hence it contains a colour class \{f,f^{\prime}\} where f\in D_{21} and f^{\prime}\in D_{13}. However f and f^{\prime} are non-incident and this yields a contradiction. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎







7 Circuit-achromatic graphic matroids M with
 2(r(M)-1) elements.




In this section, we prove Theorem 2 iii) when M is graphic. This was the most difficult challenge in this paper. The proof is broken into two parts. The first part, which is the bulk of the proof,
rests on proving the next theorem.









7.1 Theorem




Let k\in[3] and let (G,c) be a simple coloured graph where \nu(G)=n and \varepsilon(G)=2(n-1)-k. Furthermore, assume that c:E(G)\rightarrow[n-1] is a 2 -bounded (n-1) -colouring of G where (G,c) is circuit-achromatic and
 A=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\} is the set of colour-singular edges in (G,c). 
Let G^{\prime} be the graph obtained from G by adding three edges e_{1},e_{2},e_{3} to G forming a triangle T and extend c to a colouring c^{\prime} for G^{\prime} where for i=1,2,3, c^{\prime}(e_{i})=n. Suppose that the edges of T\cup A do not induce a subgraph isomorphic to K_{4} in G^{\prime}. 
Then there is a pair of edge-disjoint rainbow cycles \{C_{1},C_{2}\} in G^{\prime} where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n+2 and for i=1,2, |C_{i}\cap T|=1. 



Proof.



We shall refer to a pair of edge-disjoint rainbow cycles C_{1},C_{2} in G^{\prime} for which |E(C_{i})\cap T|=1,\ i=1,2 and |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n+2 as a near- \mathbf{T} -short rainbow cycle pair (near T -SRCP). It follows by Lemma 5 that the theorem holds for k=1. Thus we may assume that 1<k\leq 3 and the theorem holds when there are fewer than k colour-singular edges. Assume that the theorem is false. Among the counterexamples, let (G,c) be a counterexample having the least number of vertices and let n=\nu(G). For i=1,\dots,n-1, let A_{i}=c^{-1}(i). We may assume that for i=1,\dots,k, A_{i}=\{a_{i}\} . For i=k+1,\dots,n-1 , let A_{i}=\{a_{i1},a_{i2}\}. By Theorem 3, the colouring c is stratified and we may assume that G is constructed as follows:

• 



For i=1,\dots,k , G_{i} is the spanning subgraph of G where E(G_{i})=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i}\} .

• 



For i=k+1,\dots,n-1, G_{i} is the spanning subgraph of G where E(G_{i})=E(G_{i-1})\cup A_{i} .

• 



For i=2,\dots,n-1 , \kappa(G_{i})=\kappa(G_{i-1})-1. 






Note that G=G_{n-1}. For i=k+1,\dots,n-1 and j=1,2, let a_{ij}=u_{ij}v_{ij} where u_{i1},u_{i2} (resp. v_{i1},v_{i2} ) belong to the same component of G_{i-1}. Let A^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i=k+1}^{n-1}A_{i}. 
We let e_{1}=x_{1}x_{2},e_{2}=x_{1}x_{3}, and e_{3}=x_{2}x_{3} 




For i=1,\dots,n-1 , define a weighting w_{i}:V(G_{i})\rightarrow\{0,1,2\} as follows:

i) 



 w_{i}(v)=0 if d_{G_{i}}(v)\geq 4 or d_{G_{i}}(v)=0. 

ii) 



 w_{i}(v)=1 if d_{G_{i}}(v)=2 and v is a rainbow vertex in G_{i} , or d_{G_{i}}(v)=3 and |c^{-1}(E_{G_{i}}(v))|=2. 

iii) 



 w_{i}(v)=2 if d_{G_{i}}(v)=1, or d_{G_{i}}(v)=2 and |c^{-1}(E_{G_{i}}(v))|=1. 






For i\in[n-1] and j\in\{0,1,2\} let V_{ij}=\{v\in V(G_{i})\ \big|\ w_{i}(v)=j\}. When i=n-1, we let V_{j}=V_{(n-1)j}. For i\in[n-1] and j\in[2], let w(G_{i})=\sum_{v\in V(G_{i})}w_{i}(v) and let V_{i2}^{j}=\{v\in V_{i2}\ \big|\ d_{G_{i}}(v)=j\}. For j\in\{1,2\}, let V_{2}^{j}=V_{(n-1)2}^{j}. 




For all i\in[n-1], let \epsilon_{i}(v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}0&\mathrm{if}\ v\in V_{i0}\\
1&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{array}\right. .









(A) If \epsilon_{n-1}(v)=1 then v\in V(T). 



Proof.



Suppose that for some vertex v, v\not\in V(T) and \epsilon_{n-1}(v)=1. 




(A.1) d_{G}(v)\geq 2. 



Proof.



Suppose that d_{G}(v)=1. Let a be the edge incident with v. Then by construction, a\in A. Let H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}/a By assumption, the lemma holds for H^{\prime} and hence it contains a near T -SRCP
which is also seen to be a near T -SRCP in G^{\prime} , a contradiction.
It follows that d_{G}(v)\geq 2. 
∎





(A.2) d_{G}(v)=3. 



Proof.



By (A.1), d_{G}(v)\geq 2. Suppose d_{G}(v)=2. Let E_{G}(v)=\{e,f\}. If c(e)=c(f) , then by assumption, the lemma holds for H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}-v and any near- T -SRCP in H^{\prime} would also be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime}. Thus
 c(e)\neq c(f) . By construction, at least one of e or f is an edge in A. We may assume that e\in A. Suppose first that e,f do not belong to a triangle.
Then H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}/e is simple and is cycle-achromatic.
Hence the lemma holds for H^{\prime} and it contains a near- T -SRCP \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} (where |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(H)+2=n+1 ).
If f\not\in E(C_{1}^{\prime})\cup E(C_{2}^{\prime}), then \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime} . Thus we may assume that f\in E(C_{1}^{\prime}). Then the circuit C_{1} in G^{\prime} where E(C_{1})=E(C_{1}^{\prime})+e is a rainbow cycle. Furthermore, C_{1} and C_{2}^{\prime} , are rainbow cycles such that |C_{1}|+|C_{2}^{\prime}|\leq n+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime}. We conclude that e and f belong to a triangle S where for some edge g , E(S)=\{e,f,g\}. Given that e\in A, we must have that c(f)=c(g). 
Thus H=G/f\backslash g is simple and cycle-achromatic. Let H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}/f\backslash g. If A\cup T induces a K_{4} in H^{\prime}, then it is straightforward to find a near T -SRCP in G^{\prime}. Thus we may assume that such subgraph does not exist.
Now the conditions of lemma are seen to hold for H and H^{\prime} and thus H^{\prime} contains a near- T -SRCP \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} (where |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\nu(H)+2=n+1 ).
If e\not\in E(C_{1}^{\prime})\cup E(C_{2}^{\prime}), then \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCT in G^{\prime} . Thus we may assume that e\in C_{1}^{\prime}. Then the cycle C_{1} in G^{\prime} where E(C_{1})=E(C_{1}^{\prime})+f is a rainbow cycle in G^{\prime} and \{C_{1},C_{i_{2}}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime} . We conclude that d_{G}(v)\geq 3 and hence d_{G}(v)=3 (since \epsilon_{n-1}(v)=1 ).
∎





From (A.2), we have that d_{G}(v)=3 . It follows that for some i\in[k+1,n-1] and edge g, 
 E_{G}(v)=A_{i}+g. For some j\in\{1,2\} , a_{ij} and g do not belong to a triangle.
We may assume that a_{i1} and g do not belong to a triangle. Let H=G/a_{i1}\backslash a_{i2} and H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}/a_{i1}\backslash a_{i2}. Then H is simple, cycle - achromatic and |c(H)|=\nu(H)-1=n-2. We may assume that A\cup T does not induce a subgraph isomorphic to K_{4} in H^{\prime}; for if such is the case, then g\in A and hence a_{i2} and g belong to no triangle of G. In this case, one can redefine H and H^{\prime} to be H=G/a_{i2}\backslash a_{i1} and H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}/a_{i2}\backslash a_{i1} . Then A\cup T does not induce a K_{4} in H^{\prime}. By the above, H^{\prime} has a near- T -SRCP \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} .
If g\not\in E(C_{1}^{\prime})\cup E(C_{2}^{\prime}) , then \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime} . Thus we may assume that g\in E(C_{1}^{\prime}) . Then the cycle C_{1} in G where E(C_{1})=E(C_{1}^{\prime})+a_{i1} is a rainbow cycle in G^{\prime} and \{C_{1},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCPin G^{\prime}. We conclude that v\in V(T). 
∎










(B) w(G)\leq 6. 



Proof.



By (7), there are at most 3 vertices v for which \epsilon_{n-1}=1. Given that for all v , w(v)\leq 2, it follows that w(G)\leq 6. 
∎










(C) No pair of vertices in V_{2}^{2} have a common neighbour.



Proof.



Suppose to the contrary that two vertices in V_{2}^{2} share a common neighbour u. By (7), we have that V_{2}^{2}\subset V(T)=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}. Without loss of generality, we may assume that \{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subseteq V_{2}^{2} where
 x_{1}=v_{i}=v_{i1}=v_{i2}, x_{2}=v_{j}=v_{j1}=v_{j2} and u=u_{i1}=u_{j1}. Suppose x_{3}=u. Then letting C_{1}=x_{1}a_{i1}x_{3}e_{2}x_{1} and C_{2}=x_{2}a_{j1}x_{3}e_{3}x_{2}, \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime}. 
Thus x_{3}\neq u. Suppose x_{3}=u_{i2}. Then letting C_{1}=x_{1}a_{i2}x_{3}e_{2}x_{1} and C_{2}=x_{1}a_{i1}ua_{j1}x_{2}e_{1}x_{1}, \{C_{1},C_{2}\} , is seen to be edge-disjoint pair of rainbow cycles
 |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|=5\leq n+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime} and hence x_{3}\not\in\{u_{i1},u_{i2}\}. By symmetry, x_{3}\not\in\{u_{j1},u_{j2}\}. This also implies that n\geq 5. 
There is a rainbow path P in G-x_{1}-x_{2} between u_{i2} and x_{3} having length at most n-3 . Let P^{\prime}=P+a_{i2}+x_{1} and let C_{1}=P^{\prime}+e_{2} . Let C_{2} be the rainbow cycle C_{2}=x_{1}a_{i1}ua_{j1}x_{2}e_{2}x_{1} .
Then C_{1},C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles in G^{\prime} where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n-1+3=n+2, implying that \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime} , a contradiction. It follows that no pair of vertices in V_{2}^{2} have a common neighbour.
∎










(D) |V_{2}^{2}|\geq 1. 



Proof.



Suppose to the contrary that V_{2}^{2}=\mathchar 31. Then G-A_{n-1}=G_{n-2} consists of two non-trivial components G_{n-2}^{1} and G_{n-2}^{2} .
We may assume that for j=1,2, u_{(n-1)j}\in V(G_{n-2}^{1}) and v_{(n-1)j}\in V(G_{n-2}^{2}). We may also assume that \{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-2}^{1}). 
If x_{3}\in V(G_{n-2}^{1}), then by assumption, G_{n-2}^{1}+T contains a near- T -SRCP which is also a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime} .
Thus x_{3}\in V(G_{n-2}^{2}). By the construction of G , each component G_{n-2}^{j},\ j=1,2 must contain at least one edge of A. We may assume that for j=1,2, a_{j}\in E(G_{n-2}^{j}). 




(D.1) \varepsilon(G_{n-2}^{1})\geq 2. 



Proof.



Suppose \varepsilon(G_{n-2}^{1})=1. Then E(G_{n-2}^{1})=\{a_{1}\} and
 a_{1}=x_{1}x_{2} . There is a rainbow path P from x_{3} to one of the vertices v_{(n-1)1},v_{(n-1)2} (which might be the trivial path) where |P|\leq\nu(G_{n-2}^{2})-1=n-3. We may assume that P terminates at v_{(n-1)1} . We can extend P to a path P^{\prime} where P^{\prime}=P+a_{(n-1)1}+u_{(n-1)1}. Here we may assume that u_{(n-1)1}=x_{1}. 
Let C_{1} be the rainbow cycle where C_{1}=x_{1}a_{1}x_{2}e_{1}x_{1} and let C_{2} be the rainbow cycle where C_{2}=P^{\prime}+e_{2}. Then C_{1} and C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles and |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq 2+(n-3)+2=n+1. Consequently, \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, contradicting our assumptions. Thus \varepsilon(G_{n-2}^{1})\geq 2. 
∎





(D.2) \varepsilon(G_{n-2}^{1})\geq 3. 



Proof.



By (D.1), \varepsilon(G_{n-2}^{1})\geq 2. Suppose \varepsilon(G_{n-2}^{1})=2. Then E(G_{n-2}^{1})\subseteq A and hence E(G_{n-2}^{1})=\{a_{1},a_{3}\} (and a_{2}\in E(G_{n-2}^{2}) ). Then G_{n-2}^{1} is a path P=w_{1}a_{1}w_{2}a_{3}w_{3}. It is seen that for i\in\{1,3\}, \epsilon(w_{i})=1. By (7), \{w_{1},w_{3}\}\subseteq V(T) and we can assume that w_{1}=x_{1} and w_{3}=x_{2} . Since x_{3}\not\in V(G_{n-2}^{1}), it follows that \epsilon(w_{2})=0 .
It follows that at least one of a_{(n-1)1} or a_{(n-1)2} is incident with w_{2}. Without loss of generality, we may assume that a_{(n-1)1} is incident with w_{2}; that is, u_{(n-1)1}=w_{2}. 




Suppose u_{(n-1)2}=u_{(n-1)1}=w_{2}. Then v_{(n-1)1}\neq v_{(n-1)2}. By Lemma 5, there exist edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{1},P_{2} in G_{n-2}^{2} where for i=1,2, P_{i} joins x_{3} to v_{(n-1)i} and |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-2}^{2})-1=n-4. Now extend each of the paths P_{i},\ i=1,2 to path P_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2 where P_{1}^{\prime}=P_{1}+a_{(n-1)1}+w_{2}+a_{1}+x_{1} and P_{2}^{\prime}=P_{2}+a_{(n-1)2}+w_{2}+a_{3}+x_{2}. Let C_{1} and C_{2} be cycles where C_{1}=P_{1}^{\prime}+e_{3} and C_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}+e_{3} . Then C_{1} and C_{2} are seen to be edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|=|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|+6\leq(n-4)+6=n+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP in G. 




From the above, we have that u_{(n-1)2}\neq u_{(n-1)1} and hence u_{(n-1)2}\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\} . Without loss of generality, we may assume that u_{(n-1)2}=x_{2}. By Lemma 5, there is rainbow path P_{2}^{\prime} in G_{n-2}^{2} from x_{3} to v_{(n-1)2} of length at most \lfloor\frac{\nu(G_{n-2}^{2})}{2}\rfloor=\lfloor\frac{n-3}{2}\rfloor\leq n-4. Let P_{1}=P and let P_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}+a_{(n-1)2}+x_{2} and let C_{1}=P_{1}+e_{1} and C_{2}=P_{2}+e_{3}. Then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq 3+n-2=n+1 and \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is seen to be a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction. Thus \varepsilon(G_{n-2}^{1})\geq 3. 
∎





Let G_{n-2}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-2}^{1}+a_{(n-1)1}+v_{(n-1)1}. Let x_{3}^{\prime}=v_{(n-1)1} and a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{(n-1)1.} Let T^{\prime} be the triangle having vertices x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}^{\prime} and e_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{2},\ e_{2}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{3}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime}=x_{2}x_{3}^{\prime}. Suppose that that the edges A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime} together with the edges of E(T^{\prime}) do not induce a K_{4}. Let G_{n-2}^{1^{\prime\prime}}=G_{n-2}^{1^{\prime}}+E(T^{\prime}). Then by assumption, G_{n-2}^{1^{\prime\prime}} has a near- T^{\prime} -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\}. 
Exactly one of these cycles must contain a_{2}^{\prime}, say C_{1}. We may assume that e_{2}^{\prime}\in C_{1}. Let P_{1}=C_{1}-e_{2}^{\prime}. There is a rainbow path P_{2} from x_{3} to x_{3}^{\prime} where |P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-2}^{2})-1. Let C_{1}^{\prime} be the rainbow cycle where E(C_{1}^{\prime})=(E(P_{1})\cup E(P_{2}))+e_{2} Now C_{1}^{\prime} and C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}|=|C_{1}|+|C_{2}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-2}^{1^{\prime}})+2+\nu(G_{n-2}^{2})-1=n+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP.




By the above, the edges of A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime}\cup E(T^{\prime}) induce a K_{4} . Likewise, the same is true if a_{(n-1)1} is replaced by a_{(n-1)2}. Then u_{(n-1)1}=u_{(n-1)2}=u and a_{1},a_{3} are incident with u and we may assume that a_{1}=x_{1}u and a_{3}=x_{2}u. Let G_{n-2}^{2^{\prime}}=G_{n-2}^{2}+u+a_{(n-1)1}+a_{(n-1)2}. By Lemma 5 there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{1} , P_{2} from x_{3} to u where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-2}^{2^{\prime}})=\nu(G_{n-2}^{2})+1. Extend P_{1} to the path P_{1}^{\prime} where P_{1}^{\prime}=P_{1}+a_{1}+x_{1}, and extend P_{2} to the path P_{2}^{\prime} where P_{2}^{\prime}=P_{2}+a_{3}+x_{2}. Let C_{1},C_{2} be the edge-disjoint rainbow cycles in G^{\prime} where C_{1}=P_{1}^{\prime}+e_{2} and C_{2}=E(P_{2}^{\prime})+e_{3}. Then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-1}^{2})+1+4\leq n+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction.
Thus V_{2}^{2}\neq\mathchar 31. 
∎










(E) |V_{2}^{2}|\geq 2. 



Proof.



From (7), we have that |V_{2}^{2}|\geq 1. Suppose that |V_{2}^{2}|=1. By (7), we may assume that V_{2}^{2}=\{x_{1}\} and v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-1)2}=x_{1}. 




(E.1) V_{(n-2)2}^{2}=\mathchar 31. 



Proof.



Suppose there exists v\in V_{(n-2)2}^{2}. Given that v\not\in V_{2}^{2} it follows that v is adjacent to x_{1} and we may assume that a_{(n-1)1}=x_{1}v. We observe that v\in V_{1} (and hence w(v)>0 ). Thus by (7), v\in V(T) and we may assume that x_{2}=v. Let C_{1} be the rainbow cycle C_{1}=x_{1}a_{(n-1)1}x_{2}e_{1}x_{1}. Noting that G_{n-2} is connected
there is a rainbow path between x_{2} and x_{3} in G_{n-2}, say P , where |P|\leq\nu(G_{n-2})-1=n-2. Let C_{2}=P+e_{3}. Now C_{i},\ i=1,2 are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq 2+n-1=n+1 and thus C_{i},\ i=1,2 
is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction. Thus V_{(n-2)2}^{2}=\mathchar 31. 
∎





By (E.1), V_{(n-2)2}^{2}=\mathchar 31 and this in turn implies that G_{n-2}-A_{n-2}=G_{n-3} has exactly two non-trivial components, say G_{n-3}^{1} and G_{n-3}^{2}. We may assume that \{u_{(n-2)1},u_{(n-2)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{1}) and
 \{v_{(n-2)1},v_{(n-2)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{2}). 
We may also assume that x_{2}\in V(G_{n-3}^{1}). We observe that each of the components G_{n-3}^{j},\ j=1,2 must contain at least one edge of A. Without loss of generality, we may assume that for j=1,2, a_{j}\in E(G_{n-3}^{j}). 




(E.2) x_{3}\in V(G_{n-3}^{1}). 



Proof.



Suppose x_{3}\in V(G_{n-3}^{2}). Assume first that u_{(n-1)1} and u_{(n-1)2} belong to different components of G_{n-3}. Without loss of generality, we may assume that u_{(n-1)1}\in V(G_{n-3}^{1}) and u_{(n-1)2}\in V(G_{n-3}^{2}). Let P_{1} be a rainbow path from x_{2} to u_{(n-1)1} in G_{n-3}^{1} and let P_{2} be a rainbow path from x_{3} to u_{(n-1)2} in G_{n-3}. Let P_{1}^{\prime}=P_{1}+a_{(n-1)1}+x_{1} and let P_{2}^{\prime}=P_{2}+a_{(n-1)2}+x_{1}. Let C_{1}=P_{1}^{\prime}+e_{1} and C_{2}=P_{2}^{\prime}+e_{2}. Then C_{i},\ i=1,2 are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where
 |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{1})+\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})+2=n+1 and hence \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime} . Thus u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2} belong to the same component (either G_{n-3}^{1} or G_{n-3}^{2}. ). Without loss of generality, we may assume that
 \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{1}). Let x_{3}^{\prime}=v_{(n-2)1} , a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{(n-2)1} , and let G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-3}^{1}+a_{(n-1)1}+a_{(n-1)2}+x_{1}+a_{2}^{\prime}+x_{3}^{\prime}. Let T^{\prime} be the triangle with vertices x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}^{\prime} and let e_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{2},\ e_{2}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{3}^{\prime},\ e_{2}^{\prime}=x_{2}x_{3}^{\prime}. Clearly, the edges of A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime}\cup E(T^{\prime}) do not induce a K_{4} in G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}}. Thus by assumption, there is a near- T^{\prime} -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} for G_{n-1}^{1^{\prime}}. 
Exactly one of these cycles contains a_{2}^{\prime} , and we may assume a_{2}^{\prime}\in E(C_{2}). Without loss of generality, we may assume that e_{2}^{\prime}\in E(C_{2}). Let P be a rainbow path in G_{n-3}^{2} from x_{3} to x_{3}^{\prime} . Then |P|\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})-1. Let C_{2}^{\prime} be the rainbow cycle where E(C_{2}^{\prime})=E(C_{2})-e_{2}^{\prime}+E(P)+e_{2}. Then |C_{2}^{\prime}|=|C_{2}|+|P|\leq|C_{2}|+\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})-1. Thus we have that




 |C_{1}|+|C_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|C_{1}|+|C_{2}|+\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})-1\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}})+2+\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})-1=n+2. 



Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}^{\prime}\} is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction. Thus x_{3}\in V(G_{n-3}^{1}). 
∎





(E.3) For some i\in\{1,2\}, \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{i}). 



Proof.



By (E.2), x_{3}\in V(G_{n-3}^{1}). Assume that u_{(n-1)1} and u_{(n-1)2} belong to different components of G_{n-3}. Without loss of generality, we may assume that u_{(n-1)1}\in V(G_{n-3}^{1}) and u_{(n-1)2}\in V(G_{n-3}^{2}). Let G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-3}^{1}+x_{1}+a_{(n-1)1}. Let a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{(n-1)1}. Suppose first that the edges of (A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime})\cup E(T) do not induce a K_{4} . Then by assumption there is a near- T -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} for G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}} and such a pair is seen to be a near- T -SRCP for G^{\prime}. Thus
the edges of (A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime})\cup E(T) induce a K_{4} . We may assume that a_{1}=u_{(n-1)1}x_{2} and a_{3}=u_{(n-1)1}x_{3}. 
There is a rainbow path P in G starting at x_{1} and containing a_{(n-1)2} which terminates at one of the vertices u_{(n-1)1},x_{2},x_{3} and we may assume that P is a shortest such path. Suppose that P terminates at u_{(n-1)1} (and does not contain x_{2} or x_{3} ). Then |P|\leq n-3. Let P_{1}=P+a_{1}+x_{2} and let P_{2}=x_{1}a_{2}^{\prime}u_{(n-1)1}a_{3}x_{3} . For i=1,2, let C_{i}=P_{i}+e_{i}. Then \{C_{1},C_{2}\} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where
 |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq(n-2)+1+3=n+2. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP. If P terminates at x_{2} or x_{3} , then a similar argument can be used. Thus u_{(n-1)1} and u_{(n-1)2} belong to the same component of G_{n-3}. 
∎





(E.4) \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{2}). 



Proof.



By (E.3), there exists i\in\{1,2\} such that \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{i}). 
Suppose \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{1}). Let G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-3}^{1}+x_{1}+A_{n-1}. By assumption, there exists a near- T -SRCP for G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}} and such is seen to be a near- T -SRCP for G^{\prime} as well.
Thus \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{2}). 
∎





By (E.4), \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-3}^{2}). 
Let x_{1}^{\prime}=v_{(n-2)1} and let a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{(n-2)1}. Let G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-3}^{1}+a_{2}^{\prime}+x_{1}^{\prime}. Let T^{\prime} be the triangle having vertices x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2},x_{3} having edges e_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2},e_{2}^{\prime}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{3} and e_{3}^{\prime}=x_{2}x_{3}. 
Assume that the edges of (A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime})\cup E(T^{\prime}) do not induce a K_{4}. Then by assumption,
 G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}} has a near- T^{\prime} -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{1^{\prime}})+2)=\nu(G_{n-3}^{1})+3 .
Exactly one of these cycles contains a_{2}^{\prime} , and we may assume that a_{2}^{\prime}\in E(C_{1}) Furthermore, we may assume e_{1}^{\prime}\in C_{1}. Let P be a rainbow path from x_{1}^{\prime} to x_{1} in G-A_{2}. Then |P|\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{2}). 
Let C_{1}^{\prime} be the cycle where E(C_{1}^{\prime})=E(C_{1})\cup E(P)-e_{1}^{\prime}+e_{1}. Then C_{1}^{\prime} and C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where
 |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{1})+3+\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})=n+2. Thus \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP for G. 
It follows that the edges of (A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime})\cup E(T^{\prime}) induce a K_{4} , and the same is true if we replace a_{(n-1)1} with a_{(n-1)2}. 
Thus u=u_{(n-1)1}=u_{(n-1)2} and a_{1},a_{3} are both incident with u (in G_{n-3}^{1} ). We may assume a_{1}=x_{2}u,\ a_{3}=x_{3}u. However, as in a previous case, letting G_{n-3}^{2^{\prime}}=G_{n-3}^{2}+u+x_{1}+A_{n-2}+A_{n-1}, there are two edge-disjoint rainbow paths P_{1},P_{2} in G_{n-3}^{2^{\prime}} from x_{1} to u where |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})+2. The paths P_{1} and P_{2} can now be extended to paths P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime} where P_{1}^{\prime}=P_{1}+a_{1}+x_{2} and P_{2}^{\prime}=P_{2}+a_{3}+x_{3}. For i=1,2, let C_{i}=P_{i}^{\prime}+e_{i}. Then C_{i},\ i=1,2 are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where




 |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|=|P_{1}^{\prime}|+|P_{2}^{\prime}|+2=|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|+4\leq\nu(G_{n-3}^{2})+6\leq n+2, 



implying that \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction. Thus |V_{(n-1)2}^{2}|\geq 2 .
∎










(F) |V_{2}^{2}|=3. 



Proof.



By (7), |V_{2}^{2}|\geq 2. Suppose that |V_{2}^{2}|=2 . Since by (7), V_{2}^{2}\subset V(T), we may assume that V_{2}^{2}=\{x_{1},x_{2}\} and x_{1}=v_{(n-1)1}=v_{(n-1)2} and x_{2}=v_{(n-2)1}=v_{(n-2)2}. By (7), x_{1} and x_{2} have no common neighbour.




(F.1) V_{(n-3)2}^{2}=\mathchar 31. 



Proof.



Suppose there exists u\in V_{(n-3)2}^{2}. Then at exactly one of x_{1} or x_{2} is adjacent to u (because u\not\in V_{2}^{2} ). We may assume that u=u_{(n-1)1}. We see that d_{G}(u)=3 and hence w_{n-1}(u)=1. It follows that u\in V(T) and u=x_{3}. There is a rainbow path P in G-x_{1} from x_{3} to x_{2} where |P|\leq n-2 . Let
 C_{1} be the cycle in G^{\prime} where C_{1}=x_{1}a_{(n-1)1}x_{3}e_{2}x_{1} and let C_{2} be the cycle where C_{2}=P+e_{3} . Then C_{i},\ i=1,2 are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq 2+(n-2)+1=n+1. 
Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP. Thus V_{(n-3)2}^{2}=\mathchar 31. 
∎





By (F.1), V_{(n-3)2}^{2}=\mathchar 31 and thus G_{n-4} has two non-trivial components G_{n-4}^{1} and G_{n-4}^{2} where
we may assume that \{u_{(n-3)1},u_{(n-3)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{1}) and \{v_{(n-3)1},v_{(n-3)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{2}). We may also assume that x_{3}\in V(G_{n-4}^{1}). By construction, each of G_{n-4}^{j},\ j=1,2 contains at least one edge of A and we may assume that for j=1,2, a_{j}\in E(G_{n-4}^{j}). 




(F.2) There exists i\in\{n-2,n-1\} and j\in\{1,2\} such that \{u_{i1},u_{i2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{j}). 



Proof.



Suppose that for all i\in\{n-1,n-2\} and j\in\{1,2\}, \{u_{i1},u_{i2}\}\not\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{j}). We may assume that for all i\in\{n-1,n-2\} and j\in\{1,2\}, u_{ij}\in V(G_{n-4}^{j}). Let P_{1} be a rainbow path in G_{n-4}^{1} from x_{3} to u_{(n-1)1} and let P_{2} be a rainbow path in G_{n-4}^{2} from u_{(n-1)2} to u_{(n-2)2}. Now extend P_{1} to a path P_{1}^{\prime}=P_{1}+a_{(n-1)1}+x_{1} and let C_{1}=P_{1}^{\prime}+e_{2}. Let C_{2} be the cycle where E(C_{2})=E(P_{2})+a_{(n-1)2}+a_{(n-2)2}+e_{1}. Then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})+1+\nu(G_{n-4}^{2})+2=n+1. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction.
∎





By (F.2), we may assume that for some j\in\{1,2\}, \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{j}). 




(F.3) \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{2}). 



Proof.



Suppose that \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{1}). 
If \{u_{(n-2)1},u_{(n-2)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{1}), then by assumption, G_{n-4}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-4}^{1}+x_{1}+x_{2}+A_{n-1}+A_{n-2} has a near- T -SRCP which is seen to be a near- T -SRCP for G^{\prime} as well. Thus \{u_{(n-2)1},u_{(n-2)2}\}\cap V(G_{n-4}^{2})\neq\mathchar 31 and we may assume that u_{(n-2)1}\in V(G_{n-4}^{2}). Let G_{n-4}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-4}^{1}+x_{1}+A_{n-1}+a_{(n-3)1}+v_{(n-3)1}. 
Let x_{2}^{\prime}=v_{(n-3)1} and let a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{(n-3)1}. Let T^{\prime} be the triangle with vertices x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3} and edges e_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{2}^{\prime} , e_{2}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{3} and e_{3}^{\prime}=x_{2}^{\prime}x_{3}. 
Clearly the edges of (A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime})\cup E(T^{\prime}) do not induce a K_{4} in G_{n-4}^{1^{\prime}}. By assumption, G_{n-4}^{1^{\prime}} has a near- T^{\prime} -SRCP C_{i},\ i=1,2. 
There is a rainbow path P from x_{2}^{\prime} to x_{2} in G_{n-4}^{2}+a_{(n-2)1}+x_{2} where |P|\leq\nu(G_{n-4}^{2}). 
Exactly one of the cycles C_{i},\ i=1,2 must contain a_{2}^{\prime} and we may assume that a_{2}^{\prime}\in E(C_{1}). 
Furthermore, we may assume that e_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{2}^{\prime}\in E(C_{1}) (the ensuing argument being very similar if e_{3}^{\prime}\in E(C_{1}) ). Let C_{1}^{\prime} be the cycle where C_{1}^{\prime}=(C_{1}-e_{1}^{\prime})+P+e_{1}. Then




 |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}|\leq|C_{1}|+|C_{2}|+|P|\leq\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})+4+\nu(G_{n-4}^{2})=n+2 



and hence \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction. Thus
 \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{2}). 
∎





By (F.3), \{u_{(n-1)1},u_{(n-1)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{2}). If \{u_{(n-2)1},u_{(n-2)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-4}^{1}), then we could argue as we did above with x_{2} in place of x_{1}. Thus we may assume that u_{(n-2)1}\in V(G_{n-4}^{2}). 




(F.4) u_{(n-2)2}\in V(G_{n-4}^{1}). 



Proof.



Suppose that u_{(n-2)2}\in V(G_{n-4}^{2}). Let G_{n-4}^{2^{\prime}}=G_{n-4}^{2}+x_{1}+x_{2}+A_{n-2}+A_{n-1}+u_{(n-3)1}+a_{(n-3)1}. Let x_{3}^{\prime}=u_{(n-3)1} and and let a_{1}^{\prime}=a_{(n-3)1}. Let T^{\prime} be the triangle with vertices x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}^{\prime} and edges e_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{2}, e_{2}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{3}^{\prime},\ e_{3}^{\prime}=x_{2}x_{3}^{\prime}. Clearly the edges of (A-a_{1}+a_{1}^{\prime})\cup E(T^{\prime}) do not induce a K_{4} . By assumption, G_{n-4}^{2^{\prime}}+E(T^{\prime}) has a near- T^{\prime} -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\}. 
For some i , a_{1}^{\prime}\in E(C_{i}) and we may assume this holds for i=1 . Furthermore, we may assume that e_{2}^{\prime}\in E(C_{1}). Let P be a rainbow path in G_{n-4}^{1} from x_{3} to x_{3}^{\prime} , where |P|\leq\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})-1. Let C_{1}^{\prime} be the cycle where
 E(C_{1}^{\prime})=E(C_{1})-e_{2}^{\prime}+E(P)+e_{2}. Then C_{1}^{\prime} and C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where




 |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-4}^{2^{\prime}})+2+\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})-1=\nu(G_{n-4}^{2})+5+\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})-1=n+2. 



Thus \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction. It follows that u_{(n-2)2}\in V(G_{n-4}^{1}). 
∎





By (F.4), u_{(n-2)2}\in V(G_{n-4}^{1}). Let P_{1} be a rainbow path between x_{3} and
 u_{(n-2)2} in G_{n-4}^{1} and let P_{2} be a rainbow path between u_{(n-1)1} and u_{(n-2)1} in G_{n-4}^{2}. Let C_{1} be the cycle where E(C_{1})=E(P_{1})+a_{(n-2)2}+e_{3} and let C_{2} be the cycle where E(C_{2})=E(P_{2})+a_{(n-1)1}+e_{1}+a_{(n-2)1}. 
Then C_{1},C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})-1+2+\nu(G_{n-4}^{2})-1+3=n+1. Thus \{C_{1},C_{2}\}, is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction.
It follows that |V_{2}^{2}|=3. 
∎





By (7) and (7), it follows that V_{2}^{2}=V(T). We may assume that for i=1,2,3, x_{i}=v_{(n-i)1}=v_{(n-i)2}. By (7), no two vertices in V(T) have a common neighbour. Suppose there exists u\in V_{(n-4)2}^{2}. Since u\not\in V(T), it follows that w(u)=0 and hence u is adjacent to at least two vertices in V(T) , a contradiction.
Thus we have that V_{(n-4)2}^{2}=\mathchar 31 and hence G_{n-5} has exactly two non-trivial components G_{n-5}^{1} and G_{n-5}^{2} where we may assume that
 \{u_{(n-4)1},u_{(n-4)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-5}^{1}) and \{v_{(n-4)1},v_{(n-4)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-5}^{2}). By construction, both G_{n-5}^{1} and G_{n-5}^{2} must contain edges of A. We may assume that for j=1,2, a_{j}\in E(G_{n-5}^{j}). 









(G) There is at most one integer i\in\{n-3,n-2,n-1\} for which u_{i1},u_{i2} belong to different components either G_{n-5}^{1} or G_{n-5}^{2} .



Proof.



Suppose there exist two distinct i\in\{n-3,n-2,n-1\} such that u_{i1},u_{i2} belong to different components either G_{n-5}^{1} or G_{n-5}^{2} . Without loss of generality, we may assume that for all i\in\{n-1,n-2\} 
 u_{i1}\in V(G_{n-5}^{1}) and u_{i2}\in V(G_{n-5}^{2}). We may assume that u_{(n-3)1}\in V(G_{n-5}^{1}). Let P_{1} be a rainbow path from u_{(n-1)1} to u_{(n-3)1} in G_{n-5}^{1} and let P_{2} be a rainbow path from u_{(n-1)2} to u_{(n-2)2} in G_{n-5}^{2}. Let C_{1} be the rainbow cycle where E(C_{1})=E(P_{1})+a_{(n-1)1}+e_{2}+a_{(n-3)1} and let C_{2} be the rainbow cycle where E(C_{2})=E(P_{2})+a_{(n-1)2}+e_{1}+a_{(n-2)2}. Then C_{1} and C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where




 |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|=|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|+6\leq\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})-1+\nu(G_{n-4}^{1})-1+6\leq n-3+4=n+1, 



and hence \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, a contradiction.
∎










(H) For all integers i\in\{n-3,n-2,n-1\}, u_{i1} and u_{i2} belong to the same component either G_{n-5}^{1} or G_{n-5}^{2} .



Proof.



By (7), there is at most one integer i\in\{n-3,n-2,n-1\} for which u_{i1},u_{i2} belong to different components either G_{n-5}^{1} or G_{n-5}^{2} . Suppose there is exactly one such i . We may assume that
for j=1,2, u_{(n-1)j}\in V(G_{n-5}^{j}). Furthermore, we may assume that for j=1,2, u_{(n-2)j}\in V(G_{n-5}^{1}). If for some j\in\{1,2\}, u_{(n-3)j}\in V(G_{n-5}^{2}), then we can construct a near- T -SRCP as was done in the proof of (7). Thus for j=1,2, u_{(n-3)j}\in V(G_{n-5}^{1}). Let a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{(n-1)1} and
let G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-5}^{1}+V(T)+a_{2}^{\prime}+A_{n-2}+A_{n-3}. Clearly the edges of (A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime})\cup E(T) do not induce a K_{4} in G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}}. By assumption, G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}} has a near- T -SRCP and such is seen to be a near T -SRCP in G^{\prime} as well, a contradiction.
∎





By (7), we may assume that for all i\in\{n-1,n-2\} and j\in\{1,2\} , u_{ij}\in V(G_{n-5}^{1}). Suppose that \{u_{(n-3)1},u_{(n-3)2}\}\subset V(G_{n-5}^{1}). 
Let G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-5}^{1}+V(T)+A_{n-3}+A_{n-2}+A_{n-3}. Clearly, A\cup E(T) does not induce a K_{4} in G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}}. Thus by assumption G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}} 
has a near- T -SRCP which is also seen to be a near- T -SRCP in G^{\prime}. Thus \{u_{(n-3)1},u_{(n-3)2}\}\subseteq V(G_{n-5}^{2}). Let a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{(n-4)1} and let
 G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}}=G_{n-5}^{1}+x_{1}+x_{2}+A_{n-1}+A_{n-2}+v_{(n-4)1}+a_{2}^{\prime}. 
Let x_{3}^{\prime}=v_{(n-4)1}. Let T^{\prime} be the triangle having vertices x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}^{\prime} and edges e_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{2},\ e_{2}^{\prime}=x_{1}x_{3}^{\prime}, and e_{3}^{\prime}=x_{2}x_{3}^{\prime}. One sees that the edges of (A-a_{2}+a_{2}^{\prime})\cup E(T^{\prime}) do not induce a K_{4} in G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}} and thus
by assumption G_{n-5}^{1^{\prime}} has a near- T^{\prime} -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} .
Let P be a rainbow path in G_{n-5}^{2}+x_{3}+a_{(n-3)1} from x_{3}^{\prime} to x_{3}. For some i\in[2] , a_{2}^{\prime}\in C_{i} and we may assume this holds for i=1. Furthermore, we may assume that e_{2}^{\prime}\in E(C_{1}). Let C_{1}^{\prime} be the cycle where E(C_{1}^{\prime})=E(C_{1})-e_{2}^{\prime}+E(P)+e_{2}. Then C_{1}^{\prime} and C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles for which




 |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}|\leq|C_{1}|+|C_{2}|+|P|\leq\nu(G_{n-5}^{1})+5+\nu(G_{n-5}^{2})=n+2 



and hence \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}\} is a near- T -SRCP, yielding final contradiction. This completes the proof of the Theorem
7.
∎





7.1 Completing the proof of Theorem 2 iii) for graphic matroids




Let M=M(G) be a simple graphic matroid where \nu(G)=n and \varepsilon(M)=\varepsilon(G)=2(n-2) . Let c be 2 -uniform (n-2) -colouring of M (and G ) where c is circuit - achromatic. Then G is connected and r(M)=n-1. 
Let G^{\prime} be the graph obtained from G by adding the edges e_{i},\ i=1,2,3 which form a 3 -cycle T in G^{\prime} where E(T) is co-independent in M(G). 
Let V(T)=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} where e_{1}=x_{1}x_{2},\ e_{2}=x_{1}x_{3}, and e_{3}=x_{2}x_{3}. 
We shall use a proof by contradiction, assuming that the Theorem 2 iii) is false and G is a minimum counterexample; that is, (M,c) has no T -SRCP. As before, for all x\in E(M) , we let x^{\prime} denote the other element in the colour class containing x. 









(I) For all v\in V(G), if d_{G}(v)\leq 2 or d_{G}(v)=3 and |c^{-1}(E_{G}(v))|=2, then v\in V(T). 



Proof.



Let v\in V(G)\backslash V(T). Assume that either d_{G}(v)\leq 2 or d_{G}(v)=3 and |c^{-1}(E_{G}(v))|=2. 




(I.1) d_{G}(v)\geq 2. 



Proof.



Suppose d_{G}(v)=1. Let E_{G}(v)=\{e\} and let H=G/e\backslash e^{\prime} and M^{\prime}=M(H) . Let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(M^{\prime}) . Then M^{\prime} is simple, \varepsilon(M)=2(r(M^{\prime})-1)=2(n-3) and c^{\prime} is a 2 -uniform (n-3) -colouring. By assumption, (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) has a T -SRCP and such is also seen to be a T -SRCP for (M,c). 
Thus d_{G}(v)\geq 2. 
∎





(I.2) d_{G}(v)=3. 



Proof.



By (I.1), d_{G}(v)\geq 2. 
Suppose d_{G}(v)=2 and let E_{G}(v)=\{e,f\}. 
Let H=G/e\backslash e^{\prime} and let M^{\prime}=M(H). Let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(M^{\prime}). 
Then M^{\prime} is simple, \varepsilon(M^{\prime})=2(r(M^{\prime})-1)=2(n-3) and c^{\prime} is a 2 -uniform (n-3) -colouring for which (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) is circuit - achromatic.
By assumption, (M,c^{\prime}) has a T -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\}. 
If f\not\in C_{1}\cup C_{2} , then \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a T -SRCP for (M,c). Thus f\in C_{1}\cup C_{2} and hence f\neq e^{\prime}. We may assume that f\in C_{1}. Then the circuit C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{1}+e is a rainbow circuit and furthermore, C_{1}^{\prime} and C_{2} are edge-disjoint and |C_{1}^{\prime}|+|C_{2}|=|C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq r(M^{\prime})+2+1=r(M)+2. This implies that \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}\} is a T -SRCP for (M,c). Thus d_{G}(v)=3 .
∎





By (I.2) we have d_{G}(v)=3 and hence |c^{-1}(E_{G}(v))|=2. Let E_{G}(v)=\{e,e^{\prime},f\}. We note that if e,f,g are the edges of a 3 -circuit in M, then and e^{\prime} and f do not belong to a 3 -circuit in M .
Because of this, we may assume that e and f do not belong to a 3 -circuit in M .
Let H=H/e\backslash e^{\prime} and let M^{\prime}=M(H). Let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(M^{\prime}). Then M^{\prime} is simple and c^{\prime} is a 2 -uniform (r(M^{\prime})-1) -colouring for which (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}) is circuit - achromatic.
By assumption, there is a T -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} for (M^{\prime},c^{\prime}). 
If f\not\in C_{1}\cup C_{2} , then \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a T -SRCP for (G,c) . Thus f\in C_{1}\cup C_{2} and we may assume that f\in C_{1}. Let C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{1}+e. Then \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}\} is seen to be
a T -SRCP for (M,c). Thus no such vertex v exists.
∎





The next assertion follows from (7.1) and that fact that \varepsilon(G^{\prime})=2n-1 and \sum_{v\in V(G^{\prime})}d_{G^{\prime}}(v)=4n-2. 









(J) There are at least two vertices v\in V(G^{\prime}) where d_{G^{\prime}}(v)\leq 3. 









(K) There exists a rainbow vertex v\in V(G)-V(T) where d_{G}(v)=3. 



Proof.



By (7.1), there exist vertices v_{i},\ i=1,2 for which d_{G^{\prime}}(v_{i})\leq 3. 
Suppose that \{v_{1},v_{2}\}\subseteq V(T). Given that G is connected, it follows that for i=1,2, d_{G^{\prime}}(v_{i})=3 
and d_{G}(v_{i})=1. For i=1,2, let E_{G}(v_{i})=\{f_{i}\}. Let H=G-v_{1}-v_{2} and let c^{\prime} be the (circuit-achromatic) colouring of H obtained by restricting c to H . If c(f_{1})\neq c(f_{2}), then |c^{\prime}(H)|=|c(G)|=n-2=\nu(H), implying that
 H contains a rainbow cycle. Thus c(f_{1})=c(f_{2}), |c^{\prime}(H)|=n-3=\nu(H)-1, and c^{\prime} is 2 -uniform. However, since H is simple, Corollary 3 implies that (H,c^{\prime}) contains a rainbow cycle, a contradiction. Thus for some i\in\{1,2\}, 
 v_{i}\not\in V(T). Now (7.1) implies that d_{G}(v_{i})=3 and v_{i} is a rainbow vertex.
∎





By (7.1), there exists a rainbow vertex v\in V(G)-V(T), where d_{G}(v)=3. Let E_{G}(v)=\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\} . We may assume that for i=1,2,3, c(f_{i})=i. Let H=G-v and let c^{\prime} be the colouring of H obtained by restricting c to H. Then |c^{\prime}(H)|=n-2 and f^{\prime}_{i},\ i=1,2,3 are the colour-singular edges in (H,c^{\prime}). 









(L) The edges of \{f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime},f_{3}^{\prime}\}\cup E(T) induce a K_{4} in H. 



Proof.



Suppose to the contrary that the edges of \{f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime},f_{3}^{\prime}\}\cup E(T) do not induce a K_{4}. Then by Theorem 7, (H,c^{\prime}) has a near- T -SRCP \{C_{1},C_{2}\} for which (E(C_{1}),E(C_{2})) is seen to be a T -SRCP for (M,c), a contradiction.
∎





By (7.1), the edges of \{f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime},f_{3}^{\prime}\}\cup E(T) induce a K_{4} in H. Then for some vertex u , \{f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime},f_{3}^{\prime}\}\subseteq E_{G}(u). We may assume that for i=1,2,3, f_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i}u. 









(M) d_{G^{\prime}}(u)\geq 4. 



Proof.



Suppose to the contrary that d_{G^{\prime}}(u)=3. Then u is a rainbow vertex in G^{\prime}. Then we can interchange the roles of u and v. Arguing with v in place of u in (7.1), we obtain that the edges of \{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}\cup E(T) induce a K_{4}. 
Now letting N be M restricted to \bigcup_{i}\{f_{i},f_{i}^{\prime}\} and c^{\prime}=c\big|E(N), it is straightforward to find a T -SRCP for (N,c^{\prime}). Thus d_{G^{\prime}}(u)\geq 4. 
∎










(N) For some i\in\{1,2,3\}, d_{G^{\prime}}(x_{i})=3. 



Proof.



Suppose to the contrary that for all i\in\{1,2,3\} d_{G^{\prime}}(x_{i})\geq 4. By (7.1), there are at least two vertices in G^{\prime} of degree at most three. Given that d_{G^{\prime}}(u)\geq 4 (by (7.1)), there is a vertex v^{\prime}\in V(G)-V(T)-u-v where d_{G}(v^{\prime})\leq 3. By a previous argument, d_{G^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})=3 and v^{\prime} is a rainbow vertex. Let E_{G}(v^{\prime})=\{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\} 
Arguing with v^{\prime} in place of v in the proof of (7.1), it follows that the edges g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime},g_{3}^{\prime} together with E(T) induce a K_{4}. Then for some vertex u^{\prime}, \{g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime},g_{3}^{\prime}\}\subseteq E_{G}(u^{\prime}) However, it is now seen that the subgraph of G induced by
 V(T)\cup\{u,u^{\prime}\} contains a rainbow cycle, a contradiction.
∎





By (7.1), for some i\in[3], d_{G^{\prime}}(x_{i})=3. Without loss of generality, we may assume that d_{G^{\prime}}(x_{1})=3. Suppose that for some j\in\{2,3\}, d_{G^{\prime}}(x_{j})=3. We may assume that d_{G^{\prime}}(x_{2})=3. Let K=G-x_{1}-x_{2} and let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(K). 
Then either |c^{\prime}(K)|=|c(G)|=n-2=\nu(K) or |c^{\prime}(K)|=n-3=\nu(K)-1 and c^{\prime} is 2 -uniform. In either case, (K,c^{\prime}) contains a rainbow cycle, a contradiction.
Thus for all j\in\{2,3\},\ d_{G^{\prime}}(x_{j})\geq 4. Suppose d_{G}(u)>4. Then given that \sum_{v\in V(G^{\prime})}d_{G^{\prime}}(v)=4n-2, G^{\prime} must have at least 3 vertices of degree 3 and hence there exists a rainbow vertex v^{\prime}\in V(G)-V(T)-u-v where d_{G}(v^{\prime})=3. Arguing as in the proof of (7.1), there is a T -SRCP for (M,c). 
Thus d_{G}(u)=4. Let K=G-u-x_{1} and let c^{\prime}=c\big|E(K). 
Then |c^{\prime}(K)|\geq n-3=\nu(K)-1. Given that K has no rainbow cycles, there is a spanning rainbow tree in K. Thus there is a rainbow path P in K between x_{2} and x_{3}. Let C_{1}=P+e_{3} and let C_{2}=x_{1}f_{1}^{\prime}uf_{2}^{\prime}x_{2}e_{1}x_{1}. Then C_{1},C_{2} are edge-disjoint rainbow cycles where |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n-2+3=n+1. Thus \{E(C_{1}),E(C_{2})\} is a T -SRCP for (M,c). This yields a final contradiction.






8 Circuit-achromatic cographic matroids M with 2(r(M)-1) elements.




In this section, we shall prove Theorem 2 iii) for cographic matroids.
Let G be a graph with n vertices having no 2 -cocycles. Let M=M^{*}(G) where \varepsilon(M)=2(r(M)-1). We may assume that G is connected. As such, r(M^{*})=n-1 and
 r(M)=\varepsilon(M)-n+1=2(r(M)-1)-n+1. It follows that r(M)=n+1 and thus \varepsilon(M)=\varepsilon(G)=2n. Let c:E(M)\rightarrow[n] be a 2 -uniform n -colouring of M such that (M,c) is circuit-achromatic; that is, G has no rainbow cocycles.
Let G^{3} be the graph where




 G\xrightarrow[v^{1}]{e^{1}}G^{1}\xrightarrow[v^{2}]{e^{2}}G^{2}\xrightarrow[v^{3}]{e^{3}}G^{3}. 



Furthermore, assume that the edges of T=\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3}\} form a co-independent 3 -cocycle in G^{3}. Extend the colouring c to G^{3} where for i=1,2,3 , c(e^{i})=n+1. 
To prove Theorem 2 iii) for cographic matroids, it will suffice to prove the following:









8.1 Theorem




There are two edge-disjoint rainbow cocycles C_{i},\ i=1,2 in (G^{3},c) where for i=1,2, e^{i}\in C_{i} and |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq r(M)+2=n+3. That is, \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a T-SRCoP for (G^{3},c). 



Proof.



We will prove the theorem using induction on n. When n=1 , G consists of two loops and one vertex. In this case, it can be shown that G^{3} has a T -SRCoP. We shall assume that n>1 and the theorem is true for graphs with
fewer than n vertices.
Let H=G^{3} and let m=\varepsilon(H)=2n+3. For j=1,2,3 , let e^{j}=u_{1j}u_{2j} where for i=1,2, the vertices in U_{i}=\{u_{i1},u_{i2},u_{i3}\} belong to the same component of H-T. Let
Let U=U_{1}\cup U_{2}. We say that a colour class \{e,e^{\prime}\} in H is non-incidental if e and e^{\prime} are non-incident. Let \xi be the number of non-incidental colour classes in H. Let R be the set of rainbow vertices in H and let \rho=|R|. 
Since (M,c) is circuit-achromatic, (G,c) has no rainbow vertices and it follows that R\subseteq U. Similar to (6.3) in Section 6.3, we have the following:









(A) (H,c) has no monochromatic digons.




It follows by (8) that there are at most n+2 vertices having an incident pair of edges of the same colour in H (where at most two pairs belong to T ). Thus we have that |R|\geq\nu(H)-(n+2)=1. 









(B) We may assume that there is at most one vertex v\in V(H) where d_{H}(v)=2. 



Proof.



Suppose there is a vertex v\in V(H) where d_{H}(v)=2. Since M is simple, G has no 2 -cocycles and hence v\in U. It follows that |E_{H}(v)\cap T|=1. If there exists another vertex v^{\prime}\in V(H) where d_{H}(v^{\prime})=2, then again we have that v^{\prime}\in U and |E_{H}(v^{\prime})\cap T|=1. 
If v and v^{\prime} are non-adjacent, then letting C_{1}=E_{H}(v) and C_{2}=E_{H}(v^{\prime}), \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is seen to be a T -SRCoP in H. If v and v^{\prime} are adjacent, then C=E_{H}(v)\triangle E_{H}(v^{\prime}) is a 2 -cocycle of H where either C\subset T or C\subset E(G) . Since by assumption T is co-independent, the former can not occur. However, the latter is also impossible since M is simple.
∎










(C) We may assume that every pair of vertices in R is adjacent.



Proof.



Suppose that v_{1},v_{2}\in R. Then \{v_{1},v_{2}\}\subseteq U and it follows that for i=1,2, |E_{H}(v_{i})\cap T|=1. Suppose first that v_{1} and v_{2} are non-adjacent. If d_{H}(v_{1})+d_{H}(v_{2})\leq n+3, then letting C_{i}=E_{H}(v_{i}),\ i=1,2, \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is seen to be a T -SRCoP in H. Suppose instead that d_{H}(v_{1})+d_{H}(v_{2})\geq n+4. 
If for all v\in V(H)-\{v_{1},v_{2}\} , d_{H}(v)\geq 3, then




 \displaystyle 2(2n+3) 
 \displaystyle=2\varepsilon(H)=\sum_{v\in V(H)}d_{H}(v)\geq n+4+3(\nu(H)-2) 




 \displaystyle=n+4+3(n+1)=2(2n+3)+1, 



yielding a contradiction. Thus there is a vertex x\in V(H) where d_{H}(x)=2. Then x\in U and |E_{H}(x)\cap T|=1. 
We may assume that E_{H}(x)=\{e^{1},e\} and x=u_{11}. Let \{e,e^{\prime}\} be the colour class containing e .
Given there is only one such vertex x (by (8)),
the above inequality also implies that d_{H}(v_{1})+d_{H}(v_{2})=n+4. 




(C.1) We may assume that x is adjacent to at most one of v_{i},\ i=1,2. 



Proof.



Suppose that x is adjacent to both v_{1} and v_{2} . We may assume that u_{21}=v_{1}. We have e=xv_{2}. Then \{e,e^{\prime}\} is a non-incidental colour class. Let C=E_{H}(\{x,v_{1},v_{2}\}). Then C is cocycle in H where |C|=d_{H}(v_{1})+d_{H}(v_{2})-2=n+2. 
Given that |c(H)|=n+1, at least one pair of edges in C , say f and f^{\prime} , have the same colour. The colour class class \{f,f^{\prime}\} is also seen to be non-incidental. It follows that \xi\geq 2 and we see that \rho\geq n+3-(n+1-2)=4. 
Thus there exists v_{3}\in R-\{x,v_{1},v_{2}\} and d_{H}(v_{3})\leq n+1. Let C_{1}=E_{H}(x) and C_{2}=E_{H}(v_{3}). Then |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq 2+n+1=3 and for i=1,2, |C_{i}\cap T|=1. It follows that \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a T -SRCoP for (H,c). 
Thus we may assume that x is adjacent to at most one of v_{i},\ i=1,2. 
∎





By (C.1) we may assume that x and v_{1} are non-adjacent and hence d_{H}(v_{1})\leq n+1. 
Let C_{1}=E_{H}(v_{1}) and let C_{2}=E_{H}(x). Then C_{i},\ i=1,2 are disjoint rainbow cocycles where |C_{i}\cap T|=1 and |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n+1+2=n+3. It follows that \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is a T -SRCoP for (H,c). 
Thus we may assume that every pair of vertices in R is adjacent.
∎










(D) We may assume that |R|\geq 2. 



Proof.



Suppose |R|<2. As noted before, there are at most n+2 incident pairs of edges of edges of the same colour in H (where at most two pairs belong to T ) and we have that |R|\geq\nu(H)-(n+2)=1. Then |R|=1 and there are exactly n+2 vertices having an incident pair of edges of the same colour and two of these vertices belong to U and have exactly two edges of T incident with them. Thus all colour classes \{e,e^{\prime}\} are incidental and the edges of T form a path which we may assume
is u_{11}e^{1}u_{21}e^{2}u_{12}e^{3}u_{23}. We may also assume that v=u_{11} is the unique rainbow vertex.
Let H_{i},\ i=1,2 be the components of H-T where for i=1,2, U_{i}\subseteq V(H_{i}). Let C_{1}=E_{H}(v) and C_{2}=E_{H}(V(H_{2})-u_{21}). Then C_{i},\ i=1,2 are seen to be a pair of edge-disjoint rainbow cocycles. If |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\leq n+3, then \{C_{1},C_{2}\} is seen to be a T -SRCoP for (H,c). Thus we may assume that |C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\geq n+4. It follows that d_{H}(v)+d_{H}(u_{21})-1=|C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\geq n+4, and hence there is at least one colour class \{e,e^{\prime}\} where e\in E_{H}(v)-e_{1} and e^{\prime}\in E_{H}(u_{21})-e_{1}-e_{2}. However,
we have e\in E(H_{1}) and e^{\prime}\in E(H_{2}) and hence \{e,e^{\prime}\} is a non-incidental colour class. This gives a contradiction.
∎










(E) We may assume that |R|\geq 3. 



Proof.



By (8), we may assume that |R|\geq 2. Suppose |R|=2 and let R=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}. By (8), we may assume that e=v_{1}v_{2} is an edge. Suppose first that e\not\in T. Then the colour class \{e,e^{\prime}\} is non-incidental. Thus \xi\geq 1 and hence 2=|R|\geq\nu(H)-(n+2-\xi)\geq\xi+1\geq 2. Thus \xi=1 and as in the proof of (8), there are exactly n+1 vertices having an incident pair of edges of the same colour and two of these vertices belong to U and have exactly two edges of T incident with them. Thus
the edges of T form a path P from v_{1} to v_{2} . However C=P+e is seen to be a cycle where E(C)\cap T=T which is not possible since |T|=3. 
It follows from the above that e\in T. Now E_{H}(R) is seen to be a cocycle in G and thus it contains two edges of the same colour. Such edges must form a non-incidental colour class and hence \xi\geq 1. Arguing as before, we see that T is a path having endvertices v_{1} and v_{2}. However, this implies that e=v_{1}v_{2}\not\in T, a contradiction.
Thus |R|\geq 3. 
∎





By (8), we may assume that |R|\geq 3. Since R\subseteq U, at most one vertex of H has two incident edges from T. Let k=|R|. By (8), each pair of vertices in R is adjacent. At most \lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor pairs of vertices in R are joined by an edge in T. Since k\geq 3, there are at least {\binom{k}{2}}-\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor\geq k-1 edges between vertices of R which do not belong to T. Consequently, there are at least k-1 non-incidental colour classes and hence \xi\geq k-1. 
It now follows that k=|R|\geq n+3-(n+1-\xi)\geq 2+(k-1)=k+1, a contradiction. This completes the proof of Theorem 8.




∎






9 The proof of Theorem 2 i)




Let M be a simple rank- n regular matroid where \varepsilon(M)=2n-1. Let c be a 2 -bounded n -colouring of M where (M,c) is circuit-achromatic. By Observation 1, (M,c) contains a unique singular element which we shall denote by e. 
Let N be a regular extension by T, a co-independent 3 -circuit in N. 
It follows from the proofs in Sections 5, 6, 7, and 8
that Theorem 2 is true for graphic and cographic matroids.
To prove that Theorem 2 i) is true for regular matroids, we shall use induction on n , where we note that the theorem is true for n=3. We may assume that N is connected and is neither graphic nor cographic. Furthermore, M is not isomorphic to R_{10} since \varepsilon(R_{10})=2r(R_{10}). It follows by Seymour’s decomposition theorem [10], that N is a 2 - or 3 -sum of matroids which are either graphic, cographic, or isomorphic to R_{10}. 




9.1 The case where N=N_{1}\oplus_{2}N_{2} 




Suppose first that N=N_{1}\oplus_{2}N_{2}. where E(N_{1})\cap E(N_{2})=\{s\} . Given that T is a 3 -circuit of N , we may assume that T\subseteq E(N_{1}). Let M_{1}=N_{1}\backslash T and M_{2}=N_{2}. For i=1,2, let n_{i}=r(M_{i}), M_{i}^{\prime}=M_{i}\backslash s, \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}=\varepsilon(M_{i}^{\prime}), and let c_{i}^{\prime}=c\big|E(M_{i}^{\prime}). We have that n=n_{1}+n_{2}-1. 




Since M is simple and (M,c) is circuit-achromatic, it follows that for i=1,2, (M_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}^{\prime}) is simple and circuit-achromatic and thus for i=1,2, \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}\leq 2n_{i}-1. 









(A) We may assume that \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}=2n_{1}-2 and \varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}=2n_{1}-1. 



Proof.



We have that 2n-1=\varepsilon(M)=\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}. Thus 2(n_{1}+n_{2}-1)-1=\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime} and hence it follows that for some i\in\{1,2\}, \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}\geq 2n_{i}-1=2r(M_{i}^{\prime})-1; that is, for some i , \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}=2n_{i}-1. If \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}=2n_{1}-1, then by assumption, Theorem 2 i) holds for (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) and T .
Then it is seen that Theorem 2 i) holds for (M,c) and T as well.
As such, we may assume that
 \varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}=2n_{2}-1 and \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}=2n_{1}-2. 
∎





By Observation 1, (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) has a unique singular element which we denote by f. 
Since |c(M_{2}^{\prime})|=n_{2}, it follows by Observation 1.1 that M_{2}^{\prime} has an s -rainbow circuit C where |C|\leq n_{2}. Extend the colouring c_{1}^{\prime} to a colouring c_{1} of M_{1} where c_{1}(s):=c(f). 
We claim that (M_{1},c_{1}) is circuit-achromatic. For if it has a rainbow circuit D , then s\in D and C\triangle D would be a rainbow circuit in (M,c) , a contradiction. Thus (M_{1},c_{1}) is circuit-achromatic. By Observation 1, (M_{1},c_{1}) contains a unique colour-singular element which we denote by g. Note that if e\in E(M_{2}^{\prime}), then f=e and g=s; otherwise, g=e. 









(B) M_{1} is simple.



Proof.



Suppose that M_{1} is not simple. Then there is an element x\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}) which is parallel with s. Since (M_{1},c_{1}) is circuit-achromatic, it follows that c_{1}(x)=c_{1}(s)=c(f). Thus x=f^{\prime} . Now M_{2}^{\prime}+f^{\prime} is a rank- n_{2} simple matroid with 2n_{2} elements where each colour class contains two elements. It follows by Corollary 3 that M_{2}^{\prime}+f^{\prime} contains a rainbow circuit, a contradiction. Thus M_{1} is simple.
∎





Given that \varepsilon(M_{1})=2n_{1}-1 and M_{1} is simple, it follows by assumption that Theorem 2 i.1) or i.2) holds for (M_{1},c_{1}) and T . Thus either




a) 



 (M_{1},c_{1}) has a T -SRCT \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} or

b) 



for all x\in T, there exists a contains a T -SRCP \{D_{1},D_{2}\} for (M_{1}-g,c_{1}) where x\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}. 



Suppose a) holds.
If s\not\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{3}D_{i} , then \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Suppose s\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{3}D_{i}. We may assume that s\in D_{1}. Then D_{1}^{\prime}=D_{1}\triangle C is seen to be a rainbow circuit and \{D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2},D_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Thus if a) holds, then Theorem 2 i.1) holds for M. 




Suppose b) holds. Let x\in T and assume \{D_{1},D_{2}\} is a T -SRCP for (M_{1}-g,c_{1}) where x\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}. If s\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}, then \{D_{1},D_{2}\} is seen to be a T -SRCP for (M-e,c). 
Suppose that s\in D_{1}\cup D_{2} where we may assume that s\in D_{1}. Then g\neq s and hence g=e . Let D_{1}^{\prime}=C\triangle D_{1}. Then \{D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}\} is seen to be a T -SRCP for (M-e,c). 
Thus if b) holds, then Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M,c). 





9.2 The case where N=N_{1}\oplus_{3}N_{2} 




Suppose now that N=N_{1}\oplus_{3}N_{2}. where E(N_{1})\cap E(N_{2})=S=\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\} . We may assume as before that T\subset E(N_{1}). Let M_{1}=N_{1}\backslash T, M_{2}=N_{2} and for i=1,2, let n_{i}=r(M_{i}), M_{i}^{\prime}=M_{i}\backslash S, \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}=\varepsilon(M_{i}^{\prime}), and let c_{i}^{\prime}=c\big|E(M_{i}^{\prime}). We have that r(M)=n=n_{1}+n_{2}-2. Let T=\{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\}. . We shall need the following lemma:









9.1 Lemma




Suppose that for some i\in\{1,2\}, |c_{i}^{\prime}(M_{i}^{\prime})|\geq n_{i}-1. Then for some j\in[3], there exists an s_{j} -rainbow circuit C for (M_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}^{\prime}) where |C|\leq n_{i} .
Furthermore, if |c_{i}^{\prime}(M_{i}^{\prime})|=n_{i}, then for any element f\in E(M_{i}^{\prime}), one can choose C so that f\not\in C. 



Proof.



It suffices to prove the lemma when |c_{1}^{\prime}(M_{1}^{\prime})|\geq n_{1}-1. There exists B\subseteq E(M_{1}) where |B|=n_{1} , s_{1}\in B , and B-s_{1} is a rainbow subset of (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}). Note that if |c_{1}^{\prime}(M_{1}^{\prime})|=n_{1}, then for any element f, we can choose B such that f\not\in B. If B is not a basis for M_{1} , then it contains a circuit C for which s_{1}\in C and hence C is an s_{1} -rainbow circuit for (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) where |C|\leq n_{1}. 
Thus we may assume that B is a basis for M_{1}. Then B+s_{2} contains a circuit C . If C\cap S\neq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}, then C is seen to be either a s_{1} - or s_{2} - rainbow circuit for (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) where |C|\leq n_{1}. Suppose that C\cap S=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}. Then
 C\triangle S is seen to be an s_{3} - rainbow circuit for (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) for which |C\triangle S|\leq n_{1}. In either case we have the desired circuit C. If |c_{1}^{\prime}(M_{1}^{\prime})|=n_{1}, then as noted above, for any element f , we can choose B so that f\not\in B and consequently f\not\in C. 
∎





We have \varepsilon(M)=2(n_{1}+n_{2}-2)-1=2n_{1}+2n_{2}-5. Thus for some i\in\{1,2\}, \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}\geq 2n_{i}-2. Given that for i=1,2 , (M_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}^{\prime}) rainbow circuit -free, we have that for i=1,2, \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}\leq 2n_{i}-1 and |c(M_{i}^{\prime})|\leq n_{i}. 




Case 0: Suppose that for some i\in[2], \varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}=2n_{i}-1. 




Suppose \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}=2n_{1}-1. By assumption Theorem 2 i) holds for (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) and T , and in this case, Theorem 2 i) is readily seen to hold for (M,c) .




Suppose \varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}=2n_{2}-1. 
Let f be the unique colour-singular element of (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}). We also have that \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}(M_{1}^{\prime})=2n_{1}-4 and (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) has 0 or 2 colour singular elements; in the former case, f=e , and in the latter, f\neq e and e,f^{\prime} are its colour singular elements.
By assumption Theorem 2 i) holds for (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) and S. Thus there is a S -SRCP \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}\} for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) where f\not\in C_{1}^{\prime}\cup C_{2}^{\prime}. We may assume that for i=1,2, s_{i}\in C_{i}^{\prime}. 
Furthermore, we may assume that if (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) has a S -SRCT, then there is a circuit C_{3}^{\prime} such that \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is a S -SRCT for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}). 
Extend c_{1}^{\prime} to a colouring c_{1} of M_{1} where for i=1,2, c_{1}(s_{i}):=0 and c_{1}(s_{3}):=c(f). Suppose (M_{1},c_{1}) has a rainbow circuit D . Then D\cap S\neq\mathchar 31. If for some i\in[2], either D\cap S=\{s_{i}\} for D\cap S_{i}=\{s_{i},s_{3}\}. In the former, D\triangle C_{i}^{\prime} is a rainbow cycle, and in the latter D\triangle S\triangle C_{3-i}^{\prime} is seen to be a rainbow cycle. If D\cap S=\{s_{3}\}, then for any s_{3} -rainbow circuit C for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}), C\triangle D is seen to be a rainbow cycle. It follows from the above that (M_{1},c_{1}) is circuit-achromatic. We also observe that M_{1} is simple; for if there is an element x\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}) which is parallel to an element of S, then M_{2}^{\prime}+x would have rank n_{2} and 2n_{2} elements and hence would have a rainbow circuit, a contradiction. By assumption Theorem 2 i) holds for (M_{1},c_{1}). 




Case 0.1 Suppose that Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M_{1},c_{1}) and \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is a T -SRCT for (M_{1},c_{1}). 




We may assume that S\cap\bigcup_{i}D_{i}\neq\mathchar 31 ; otherwise, \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is a T -SRCT for (M,c). 




Case 0.1.1 Suppose e\in E(M_{2}^{\prime}). 




We have that f=e . Suppose first that Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}). Then by assumption there is a circuit C_{3}^{\prime} such that
 \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is a S -SRCT for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) (where s_{3}\in C_{3}^{\prime} ).
Suppose first that for all i\in[3], |D_{i}\cap T|\leq 1. We may assume for all i\in[3] that if D_{i}\cap T\neq\mathchar 31, then D_{i}\cap T=\{s_{i}\}. 
For i=1,2,3 let C_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{i}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{i}\cap T=\mathchar 31\\
D_{i}\triangle C_{i}^{\prime}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{i}\cap T\neq\mathchar 31.\end{array}\right. 
Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyT for (M,c). Suppose instead that for some i\in[3], |D_{i}\cap T|=2. We may assume that D_{1}\cap T=\{s_{1},s_{3}\}. 
We may assume that D_{3}\cap T=\mathchar 31. If D_{2}\cap T=\mathchar 31, then letting C_{1}=D_{1}\triangle S\triangle C_{2}^{\prime},C_{2}=D_{2}, and C_{3}=D_{3}, then it is seen that \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is a T -SRCyT for (M,c). 
Suppose D_{2}\cap T\neq\mathchar 31. Then D_{2}\cap T=\{s_{2}\}. Clearly e is not parallel with s_{2} , for otherwise, C_{2}^{\prime}-s_{2}+e is a rainbow circuit in (M,c). Since Theorem 2 ii) holds for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}), 
there is an s_{2} -semi-SRCP \{C_{1}^{\prime\prime},C_{2}^{\prime\prime}\} for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}). Let C_{1}=D_{1}\triangle S\triangle C_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ C_{2}=D_{2}\triangle C_{2}^{\prime\prime} and C_{3}=D_{3}. Then \{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyT for (M,c). 
Thus Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M,c). 




Suppose instead that Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}). We may assume that for all i\in[3],\ |D_{i}\cap S|\leq 1; otherwise, the previous arguments show that there is S -SRCT for (M,c). 
We may also assume that for all i\in[3], if D_{i}\cap S\neq\mathchar 31, then D_{i}\cap S=\{s_{i}\}. For all 1\leq i<j\leq 3 there exists a S -SRCP \{C_{i}^{ij},C_{j}^{ij}\} for (M_{2}^{\prime}-e,c_{2}^{\prime}) where s_{i}\in C_{i}^{ij} and s_{j}\in C_{j}^{ij}. 
For all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, let D_{i}^{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{i}\triangle C_{i}^{ij}&\mathrm{if}\ s_{i}\in D_{i}\\
D_{i}&\mathrm{if}\ s_{i}\not\in D_{i}\end{array}\right. and D_{j}^{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{j}\triangle C_{j}^{ij}&\mathrm{if}\ s_{j}\in D_{j}\\
D_{j}&\mathrm{if}\ s_{j}\not\in D_{j}.\end{array}\right. Then \{D_{i}^{ij},D_{j}^{ij}\} is seen to be a S -SRCyP for (M-e,c) and hence Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M,c). 




Case 0.1.2: Suppose that e\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}). 




Then e and f^{\prime} are the colour singular elements of (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) and |c_{1}^{\prime}(M_{1}^{\prime})|=n_{1}-1. By Lemma 9.2, for some j\in[3] , there is an s_{j} -rainbow circuit D for (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) where |D|\leq n_{1}. 
Then D must be a s_{3} -rainbow circuit; for otherwise, D\triangle C_{1}^{\prime} or D\triangle C_{2}^{\prime} is a rainbow cycle. If Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}), then there is an s_{3} -rainbow circuit C for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) where f\not\in C. As such, C\triangle D is seen to be
a rainbow cycle in (M,c) , a contradiction. Thus Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) and by assumption, there is a circuit C_{3}^{\prime} such that \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is a S -SRCT for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) (where s_{3}\in C_{3}^{\prime} ).
Extend the colouring c_{1}^{\prime} to a colouring c_{1} of M_{1} where c_{1}(s_{3}):=c(f) and for i=1,2, c_{1}(s_{i}):=0. 
As in Case 0.1.1, M_{1} is simple and (M_{1},c_{1}) is circuit-achromatic. Now using the circuits C_{i}^{\prime},D_{i},\ i=1,2,3 one can construct a T -SRCT for (M,c) using a similar argument as in Case 0.1.1.




Case 0.2: Suppose that Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M_{1},c_{1}) and T .




Let g be the unique colour-singular element of (M_{1},c_{1}), noting that either g=e or g=s_{3} , depending on whether e\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}) or e\in E(M_{2}^{\prime}). By assumption, for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, there is a T -SRCP \{D_{i}^{ij},D_{j}^{ij}\} for (M_{1}-g,c_{1}) where t_{i}\in D_{i}^{ij} and t_{j}\in D_{j}^{ij}. 




Case 0.2.1 Suppose e\in E(M_{2}^{\prime}). 




We observe that g=s_{3} and f=e. 
By the choice of C_{i}^{\prime},\ i=1,2, e\not\in C_{1}^{\prime}\cup C_{2}^{\prime}. For all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, let C_{i}^{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{i}^{ij}\triangle C_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{i}^{ij}\cap S=\{s_{i^{\prime}}\}\\
D_{i}^{ij}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{i}^{ij}\cap S=\mathchar 31\end{array}\right. and
 C_{j}^{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{j}^{ij}\triangle C_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{j}^{ij}\cap S=\{s_{j^{\prime}}\}\\
D_{j}^{ij}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{j}^{ij}\cap S=\mathchar 31.\end{array}\right. Then \{C_{i}^{ij},C_{j}^{ij}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyP for (M-e,c) where t_{i}\in C_{i}^{ij} and f_{j}\in C_{j}^{ij}. 




Case 0.2.2 Suppose e\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}). 




We have that g=e . It suffices to show that \{D_{1}^{12},D_{2}^{12}\} can be extended to a T -SRCP \{C_{1}^{12},C_{2}^{12}\} for (M-e,c) where for i=1,2, t_{i}\in C_{i}^{12}. We may assume that S\cap(D_{1}^{12}\cup D_{2}^{12})\neq\mathchar 31. 
Suppose first that for some i\in\{1,2\}, |D_{i}^{12}\cap S|=2. We may assume that D_{1}^{12}\cap S=\{s_{1},s_{3}\}. If D_{2}^{12}\cap S=\mathchar 31, then let C be a s_{2} -rainbow circuit for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) and let
 C_{1}^{12}=D_{1}^{12}\triangle S\triangle C and C_{2}^{12}=D_{2}^{12}. Then \{C_{1}^{12},C_{2}^{12}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyP for (M-e,c) . Suppose instead that D_{2}^{12}\cap S=\{s_{2}\}. Since Theorem 2 ii) holds for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) and f is not parallel with s_{2} (otherwise, C_{2}^{\prime}-s_{2}+f is a rainbow circuit), there exists a s_{2} -semi-SRCP \{C_{1}^{\prime\prime},C_{2}^{\prime\prime}\} for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) where we may assume that f\not\in C_{2}^{\prime\prime}. Letting C_{1}^{12}=D_{1}^{12}\triangle S\triangle C_{1}^{\prime\prime} and C_{2}^{12}=D_{2}^{12}\triangle C_{2}^{\prime\prime} , we see that \{C_{1}^{12},C_{2}^{12}\} is a T -SRCyP for (M-e,c) .




By the above, we may assume that for i=1,2, |D_{i}^{12}\cap S|\leq 1. Suppose first that (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) has a S -SRCT. Then by assumption, there is a circuit C_{3}^{\prime} where \{C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}\} is a T -SRCT for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}). 
For i=1,2, let




 C_{i}^{12}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{i}^{12}\triangle C_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{i}^{12}\cap S=\{s_{i^{\prime}}\}\\
D_{i}^{12}&\mathrm{if}\ D_{i}^{12}\cap S=\mathchar 31.\end{array}\right. 



Then \{C_{1}^{12},C_{2}^{12}\} is seen to be a T -SRCyP for (M-e,c). 
Suppose now that Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}). Then for all 1\leq i<j\leq 3, there exists a S -SRCP \{F_{i}^{ij},F_{j}^{ij}\} for (M_{2}^{\prime}-f,c_{2}^{\prime}) where f\not\in F_{i}^{ij}\cup F_{j}^{ij}. 
Suppose that D_{1}^{12}\cap S=\{s_{i}\} and D_{2}^{12}\cap S=\{s_{j}\}. Letting C_{1}^{12}=D_{1}^{12}\triangle F_{i}^{ij} and C_{2}^{12}=D_{2}^{12}\triangle F_{j}^{ij}, we see that \{C_{1}^{12},C_{2}^{12}\} is a T -SRCyP for (M-e,c). 
Suppose that for some i\in[2],\ D_{i}^{12}\cap S=\mathchar 31. We may assume that D_{2}^{12}\cap S=\mathchar 31. Then for some i\in[2],\ D_{1}^{12}\cap S=\{s_{i}\}. Let C=F_{i}^{ij} , where j\neq i. Letting C_{1}^{12}=D_{1}^{12}\triangle C and C_{2}^{12}=D_{2}^{12}, we seen that \{C_{1}^{12},C_{2}^{12}\} is a T -SRCyP for (M-e,c). 




From the above, we may assume that \varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\leq 2n_{2}-2 and \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\geq 2n_{1}-3 . As such, there are two remaining cases where either \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}=2n_{1}-2 or \varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}=2n_{2}-2. 
If for some i , \varepsilon_{i}=2n_{i}-2, then n_{i}-1\leq|c_{i}^{\prime}(M_{i}^{\prime})|\leq n_{i}. We say that (M_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}^{\prime}) is of type 1 or type 2, depending on whether |c_{i}^{\prime}(M_{i}^{\prime})|=n_{i}-1 or |c_{i}^{\prime}(M_{i}^{\prime})|=n_{i}. 




Case 1: Suppose \varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}=2n_{1}-2. 




Case 1.1 (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) is of type 1.




We have that |c(M_{1}^{\prime})|=n_{2}-1 and hence c_{1}^{\prime} is 2 -uniform and e\in E(M_{2}^{\prime}). We also have that \varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}=2n_{2}-3. By Lemma 9.2, there exists j\in[3] for which there is an s_{j} -rainbow circuit C for (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) where |C|\leq n_{2}. We may assume this is true for j=1. Extend c_{1}^{\prime} to a colouring c_{1}^{\prime\prime} of M_{1}^{\prime\prime}=M_{1}^{\prime}+s_{1} where c_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s_{1}):=0. If (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) contains a rainbow circuit, say D , then s_{1}\in D and hence C\triangle D is a seen to be a rainbow cycle of (M,c) . Thus (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) is circuit-achromatic and hence no element of E(M_{1}^{\prime}) is parallel with s_{1}, implying that M_{1}^{\prime\prime} is simple. We also have that \varepsilon(M_{1}^{\prime\prime})=2n_{1}-1 and s_{1} is the unique colour-singular element of (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}). 
By assumption, Theorem 2 i) holds for (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) and T and either

a) 



 (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) has a T -SRCT \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} or

b) 



For all x\in T , (M_{1}^{\prime\prime}-s_{1},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) has a T -SRCP \{D_{1},D_{2}\} where x\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}. 



Suppose a) occurs. If s_{1}\not\in\bigcup_{i\in[3]}D_{i}, then \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). Thus we may assume that s_{1}\in\bigcup_{i\in[3]}D_{i} and more specifically, s_{1}\in D_{1} .
Let C_{1}=C\triangle D_{1}. Since |C|\leq n_{2}, it is seen that \{C_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is a T -SRCyT for (M,c) . Thus if a) holds, then Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M,c). 




Suppose b) occurs. Let x\in T. Then (M_{1}^{\prime\prime}-s_{1},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) has a T -SRCP \{D_{1},D_{2}\} where x\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}. Then \{D_{1},D_{2}\} is seen to be a T -SRCP for (M-e,c). Thus if b) holds, then
Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M,c). 




Case 1.2 (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) is of type 2.




We have that (M_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}) contains exactly two colour-singular elements which we denote by f and g. We shall consider two sub-cases depending on whether e\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}) or e\in E(M_{2}^{\prime}). 




Case 1.2.1 e\in E(M_{2}^{\prime}). 




We see that (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) has exactly three singular elements which are seen to be e,f^{\prime} and g^{\prime} . Also, |c_{2}^{\prime}(M_{2}^{\prime})|=n_{2}. 
By Lemma 9.2, there exists j\in[3] for which (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) has an s_{j} -rainbow circuit C where |C|\leq n_{2}. Moreover, C can be chosen such that either f^{\prime}\not\in C or g^{\prime}\not\in C. We may assume that C is an s_{1} -rainbow circuit.
Let M_{1}^{\prime\prime}=M_{1}^{\prime}+s_{1} and define a colouring c_{1}^{\prime\prime} for M_{1}^{\prime\prime} where c_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s_{1}):=0, c_{1}^{\prime\prime}(g):=c(f) and for all x\in E(M_{1}^{\prime})-g, define c_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x):=c_{1}^{\prime}(x). Note that s_{1} is the unique colour-singular element of (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}). 
We claim that (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) is circuit-achromatic. For suppose it contains a rainbow circuit, say D . Then s_{1}\in D. If \{f,g\}\cap D=\mathchar 31, then C\triangle D is seen to be a rainbow cycle of (M,c). Thus \{f,g\}\cap D\neq\mathchar 31 . We may assume that f\in D. As mentioned above, we can choose C so that f^{\prime}\not\in C. It now follows that C\triangle D is a rainbow cycle of (M,c). Thus (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) is circuit-achromatic and hence no edge of E(M_{1}^{\prime}) is parallel with s_{1} and M_{1}^{\prime\prime} is simple.
We can now argue as in Case 1.1 where it is seen that either a) or b) is true.
Suppose that a) is true.
If s_{1}\not\in\bigcup_{i}D_{i}, then \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Suppose s_{1}\in\bigcup_{i}D_{i} where we may assume s_{1}\in D_{1}. If f\in D_{1} (resp. g\in D_{1} ), then choose C such that f^{\prime}\not\in C (resp. g^{\prime}\not\in C ).
Let C_{1}=C\triangle D_{1}. Then \{C_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). Thus if a) is true, then Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M,c). 
Suppose b) is true. Let x\in T. Then (M_{1}^{\prime\prime}-s_{1},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) has a T -SRCP \{D_{1},D_{2}\} where x\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2} and \{D_{1},D_{2}\} is also seen to be a T -SRCP in (M-e,c). In this case, Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M,c). 




Case 1.2.2 e\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}). 




We have that M_{1}^{\prime} contains exactly two colour-singular elements, one which is e and the other being say f. Then (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) has exactly one colour-singular element, namely f^{\prime}. 
As in Case 1.2.1, there exists j\in[3] for which (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) has an s_{j} -rainbow circuit C where |C|\leq n_{2}. We may assume that this true for j=1. 
Extend the colouring c_{1}^{\prime} to a colouring c_{1}^{\prime\prime} of M_{1}^{\prime\prime}=M_{1}^{\prime}+s_{1} where c_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s_{1}):=c(f). If (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) contains a rainbow circuit, say D , then s_{1}\in D and C\triangle D is seen to be a rainbow cycle in (M,c). Thus (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) is circuit-achromatic. Suppose that M_{1}^{\prime\prime} is not simple. Then there exists x\in E(M_{1}^{\prime}) where x is parallel with s_{1}. Since (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) is circuit-achromatic, c_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x)=c_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s_{1})=c(f). Thus x=f. Now redefine M_{i}\ i=1,2 so that f\in E(M_{2}). In this case, \varepsilon(M_{2}^{\prime})=2n_{2}-2 and |c_{2}^{\prime}(M_{2}^{\prime})|=n_{2}-1 and we can revert back to Case 1.1 with the roles of M_{1} and M_{2} switched. Thus we may assume that M_{1}^{\prime\prime} is simple.
By assumption, Theorem 2 i) holds for (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) and T. Suppose Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}). Let \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} be a T -SRCT for (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}). 
If s_{1}\not\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{3}D_{i} , then \{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\} is seen to be a T -SRCT for (M,c). 
Suppose s_{1}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{3}D_{i} where we may assume s_{1}\in D_{1}. Letting D_{1}^{\prime}=D_{1}\triangle C it is seen that \{D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2},D_{3}\} is a T -SRCyT for (M,c). Thus we see that Theorem 2 i.1) holds for (M,c). 
Suppose Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}). 
Note that e is the unique colour-singular element in (M_{1}^{\prime\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}) . Let x\in T. Then there is a T -SRCP \{D_{1},D_{2}\} for (M_{1}^{\prime\prime}-e,c_{1}^{\prime\prime}). If s_{1}\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}, then \{D_{1},D_{2}\} is seen to be a T -SRCP for (M-e,c). If s_{1}\in D_{1}\cup D_{2} and say s_{1}\in D_{1}, then let D_{1}^{\prime}=D_{1}\triangle C. Then it is seen that \{D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}\} is a T -SRCyP for (M-e,c) where x\not\in D_{1}^{\prime}\cup C_{2}. Thus Theorem 2 i.2) holds for (M,c). 




Case 2: Suppose \varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}=2n_{2}-2. 




Case 2.1 (M_{2}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}) is type 1.
